Engineering topological flat bands in Γ \Gamma -valley moiré with Ising-type SOC: twisted 1T– ZrS 2 \text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}} and 1T– SnSe 2 \text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
Hanqi Pi, Yves H. Kwan, Haoyu Hu, Yi Jiang, Dumitru Călugăru, Jie Shan, Kin Fai Mak, Miguel M. Ugeda, Dmitri K. Efetov, Maia G. Vergniory, B. Andrei Bernevig

Abstract
Twisted moiré superlattices hosting topological flat bands provide a platform to explore the interplay between topology and correlations. Here we investigate topological band structures in Γ \Gamma -valley moiré systems based on 1T– ZrS 2 \text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}} and 1T– SnSe 2 \text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}} . Using large-scale ab initio calculations and continuum modelling, we demonstrate that both materials exhibit an approximate spin- U ⁡ ( 1 ) \mathrm{U}(1) symmetry and host isolated topological moiré valence bands, including quantum spin Hall and high spin Chern states. By constructing a hierarchy of Γ \Gamma -valley moiré continuum models, we show that isolated moiré bands carry a trivial C 3 C_{3} symmetry indicator when the low-energy physics is described by a single effective orbital and a single layer-hybridized branch (either bonding or antibonding). Topological bands therefore arise from the inter-branch and/or inter-orbital coupling. Moreover, we determine the interaction-driven phase diagrams using Hartree–Fock and exact diagonalization, finding various phases tunable by twist angle, interaction strength, and displacement field. We identify specific conditions under which fractional Chern insulators are favored. Together with previous work showing that the moiré conduction bands of 1T– ZrS 2 \text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}} and 1T– SnSe 2 \text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}} realize M M -valley twisting and host quasi-one-dimensional physics Călugăru et al. 2025 , our results establish these systems as ideal platforms for strongly correlated moiré physics and provide a systematic framework for understanding topological band structures in Γ \Gamma -valley moiré materials.



I
Introduction

Moiré superlattices have become a highly tunable platform for exploring correlated and topological quantum matter, with bandwidth, correlations, and topology controlled by twist angle, displacement field, and carrier density
Dai et al. 2016
;
Andrei et al. 2021
;
Andrei and MacDonald 2020
;
Koshino and Son 2019
;
Balents et al. 2020
;
Chen et al. 2019a
. Experiments have realized a wide variety of correlated phases, including unconventional superconductivity in graphene moiré systems
Cao et al. 2018a
;
Cao et al. 2018b
;
Yankowitz et al. 2019
;
Sharpe et al. 2019
;
Lu et al. 2019
;
Serlin et al. 2020
;
Po et al. 2018
;
Cao et al. 2020
;
Chen et al. 2019b
, as well as Mott insulators, generalized Wigner crystals
Regan et al. 2020
;
Li et al. 2021
;
Huang et al. 2021
;
Xu et al. 2020
;
Tang et al. 2020
, and superconductivity
Xia et al. 2025
;
Guo et al. 2025
;
Xu et al. 2025
in TMD moiré heterostructures. The nontrivial band topology of moiré flat bands further intertwines with correlations
Wu et al. 2019a
;
Dong et al. 2023
;
Xu and Balents 2018
;
Herzog-Arbeitman et al. 2024
to produce phenomena such as the fractional Chern insulators (FCIs) at zero magnetic field
Regnault and Bernevig 2011
;
Sun et al. 2011
;
Neupert et al. 2011
;
Wang et al. 2024
;
Cai et al. 2023
;
Park et al. 2023
;
Zeng et al. 2023
;
Xie et al. 2021
;
Xie et al. 2025
and the fractional quantum spin Hall insulator
Kang et al. 2024
observed in twisted
\text{MoTe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, although more work is needed to determine the origin of experimental observation. Exploring and understanding new moiré platforms that naturally host isolated topological flat bands is a key step toward robustly engineering these exotic correlated and topological quantum phases.
Research has extended moiré physics beyond the familiar
K
-valley to platforms whose low-energy states reside at other momenta and in distinct symmetry environments
Jiang et al. 2024
;
Wang et al. 2022
;
Kennes et al. 2020
;
Claassen et al. 2022
;
Klebl et al. 2022
. These include
\Gamma
-valley dominated regimes in TMD homobilayers and twisted double bilayers
Angeli and MacDonald 2021
;
Pei et al. 2022
;
Foutty et al. 2023
;
Campbell et al. 2024
;
Ma et al. 2025
;
Qi et al. 2025
, as well as
M
-valley systems that realize quasi-one-dimensional or highly anisotropic triangular/rectangular moiré lattices
Călugăru et al. 2025
;
Lei et al. 2025
;
Bao et al. 2025
. Although these non-
K
-valley platforms broaden the accessible correlation physics, the single-particle moiré bands are often topologically trivial
Angeli and MacDonald 2021
;
Qi et al. 2025
.
This motivates the search for new moiré materials that intrinsically host topological minibands. In the weak-coupling regime, several works have proposed systematic theoretical frameworks based on symmetry-based continuum modelling and topological quantum chemistry to predict and diagnose topological moiré flat bands
Crépel and Cano 2025
;
Lhachemi et al. 2025
;
Liu et al. 2025
;
Yang et al. 2025
.

. (a) Top and side views of the 1T-TMD crystal structure. (b, c) Band structures of monolayer (b)
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and (c)

calculated without (left subpanels) and with (right subpanels) spin-orbit coupling (SOC). The red and blue dots represent the projection weights of the chalcogen in-plane (
p_{x},p_{y}
) and out-of-plane
p_{z}

\Gamma
states. Top: The state contributed by
p_{z}
orbital transforms as a 1D IRREP (left) without SOC, which preserves an approximate spin-
\mathrm{SU}(2)
symmetry near
\Gamma
and evolves into a single degenerate Kramers pair upon including SOC (right). Bottom: The state contributed by
(p_{x},p_{y})
orbital transforms as a 2D IRREP (left) without SOC, which preserves an Ising-type SOC near
\Gamma
and splits into two energetically separated Kramers pairs upon including SOC (right).

In this work, we use twisted bilayer 1T-
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and 1T-
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
as concrete material platforms and study the origin of topological moiré bands in
\Gamma
-valley moiré system. Both systems host topological moiré valence bands, including quantum spin Hall (QSH) and high-spin Chern states, as established by large-scale density functional theory (DFT) calculations and continuum modelling. The QSH state in AB-stacked twisted 1T-
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
has been reported in Ref.
Claassen et al. 2022
, where the topology was attributed to strong spin-orbit coupling (SOC) and an emergent kagome lattice. Here we study both AA- and AB-stacked twisted 1T-
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and 1T-
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and develop a symmetry-based microscopic framework to understand isolated topological moiré bands in systems with Ising-type SOC. Based on the symmetry and orbital analysis of the monolayer, we identify the origin of an approximate spin-
\mathrm{U}(1)
symmetry in the low-energy states of twisted bilayer
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
. Because
\Gamma
-valley states in the two layers remain momentum-aligned under twisting, interlayer tunneling is generally strong and naturally forms layer-hybridized bonding and antibonding branches. Hereafter, “branch” refers to these bonding/antibonding layer-hybridized states. By analyzing a hierarchy of symmetry-constrained continuum models, we show that isolated topological bands in spin-
\mathrm{U}(1)
-symmetric moiré systems require inter-branch and/or inter-orbital coupling. Finally, using Hartree–Fock and exact diagonalization (ED), we observe spin-polarized Chern states and QSH states, and discuss the conditions required to realize FCIs in these platforms. Our framework provides a symmetry- and material-based route to systematically analyze and design topological moiré bands based on 1T-TMDs, which can be straightforwardly generalized to other moiré and superlattice platforms.

II

\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}

. The left and right subpanels correspond to the LDOS of the top and bottom layers, respectively.

We first examine the monolayer band structures of 1T–
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and 1T–
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
. Both materials crystallize in a hexagonal lattice as shown in
fig.

1(a)
and belong to the space group (SG)
P\overline{3}m1
(No. 164). In this work, we focus on the valence bands, and the analysis of the conduction bands can be found in Ref.
Călugăru et al. 2025
. As shown in
figs.

1(b)
and
1(c)
, the monolayer valence band maxima (VBM) of both compounds lie at
\Gamma
(in
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, SOC shifts the VBM from the
\Gamma
–
M
line to
\Gamma
), and are dominated by chalcogen
(p_{x},p_{y})
orbitals. Crucially, the topmost state at
\Gamma
transforms according to a 2D irreducible representation (IRREP) without SOC, and the inclusion of SOC splits it into two Kramers pairs. Although both compounds have the same crystalline symmetry and chalcogen
(p_{x},p_{y})
-dominated
\Gamma
-valley valence states, the two Kramers pairs are assigned to different IRREPs. In
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
the split spinful
\Gamma
states transform as
\overline{\Gamma}_{6}\overline{\Gamma}_{7}
and
\overline{\Gamma}_{9}
with odd parity under inversion, while in
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
they transform as
\overline{\Gamma}_{4}\overline{\Gamma}_{5}
and
\overline{\Gamma}_{8}
with even parity. The IRREP notation follows the convention of
Bilbao Crystallographic Server
(BCS)
Aroyo et al. 2011
;
Aroyo et al. 2006a
;
Aroyo et al. 2006b
.
The
\overline{\Gamma}_{6}\overline{\Gamma}_{7}
(
\overline{\Gamma}_{4}\overline{\Gamma}_{5}
) pair corresponds to
p
-like effective orbital with total angular momentum
|J_{z}|=\frac{3}{2}
and
\overline{\Gamma}_{9}
(
\overline{\Gamma}_{8}
) exhibits
s
-like character with
|J_{z}|=\frac{1}{2}
. The different IRREPs in the two compounds can be understood from the fact that the
\Gamma
-valley states arise from the hybridization between
(p_{x},p_{y})
orbitals on the two chalcogen sublayers through the intermediate metal sublayer, which forms combinations with even- and odd-parity since the two sublayers are related by
\mathcal{I}
. Because the chalcogen orbitals hybridize with different metal-orbitals,
i.e.
,
Zr
-
d
in
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
Sn
-
s
in
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, the relative ordering of these two combinations can be reversed, leading to different IRREP assignments for the topmost Kramers pairs.

The orbital component at
\Gamma
plays a central role in determining the effective spin symmetry of the low-energy moiré bands.
Depending on the orbital character, the resulting moiré low-energy states can have either approximate spin-
\mathrm{U}(1)
or spin-SU(2) symmetry.
To see this, we project the onsite atomic SOC,
H_{\mathrm{SOC}}=\lambda\mathbf{L}\cdot\mathbf{S}=\frac{\lambda}{2}\left(2L_{z}S_{z}+L_{+}S_{-}+L_{-}S_{+}\right)
,
onto the low-energy
(p_{x},p_{y})
manifold. Introducing the circular basis
\ket{p_\pm}=\frac{\ket{p_x}\pm i\ket{p_y}}{\sqrt{2}}
for the topmost
\Gamma
-state without SOC, the ladder operators
L_{\pm}
connect the low-energy
p_{\pm}
sector to the higher-lying
p_{z}
sector and therefore have no matrix elements within the projected subspace. Thus, within the low-energy
(p_{x},p_{y})
manifold, the SOC is diagonal in
S_{z}
and is therefore Ising-like, which splits the doubly degenerate states without SOC into two spinful Kramers doublets as shown in bottom panel of
fig.

1(d)
.
By contrast, if the low-energy
\Gamma
-valley manifold is dominated by an
m_{\ell}=0
orbital such as
d_{z^{2}}
in
\text{MoTe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, the projected first-order SOC vanishes and spin anisotropy arises only through virtual mixing with remote bands. This is the origin of the approximate spin-
\mathrm{SU}(2)
symmetry of the
\Gamma
-valley moiré bands in bilayer and multilayer twisted
\text{MoTe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}

Qi et al. 2025
;
Jia et al. 2024
. In the exact spin-
\mathrm{SU}(2)
limit with time-reversal symmetry, the
Z_{2}
index is trivial.

Because the monolayer structure lacks the
C_{2z}
symmetry, the untwisted bilayer structure allows for two distinct configurations. The first, referred to as AA stacking, is constructed by placing one monolayer directly on top of the other and preserves the symmetry of the monolayer
i.e.
,
P\overline{3}m1
(No. 164). The second, referred to as AB stacking, is obtained by applying
C_{2z}
to the bottom layer and belongs to SG
P\overline{6}m2
(No. 187). Upon twisting, the inversion symmetry
\mathcal{I}
(or in-plane mirror
\mathcal{M}_{z}
) present in the untwisted AA (AB) configuration is broken, while the
C_{3z}
and in-plane
C_{2}
about [010] ([210]) are preserved. As shown in
figs.

2(a)
,
2(b)
and
2(c)
, the large-scale DFT calculations at twist angle
3.89^{\circ}
show that AB–
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
hosts a single isolated set of bands composed of the top two spinful valence bands. By contrast, AA-
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
does not exhibit an isolated valence-band manifold for
\theta\geq 3.89^{\circ}
(see
fig.

S8
), and we therefore do not further discuss it. Twisted AA– and AB–
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
instead organize the top six spinful bands into three isolated sets. In all cases, the isolated band sets exhibit approximately double degeneracy along high-symmetry lines. By inspecting the DFT wavefunctions, we find that spin-
\mathrm{U}(1)
remains an excellent approximate symmetry for these low-energy isolated bands, with symmetry breaking below 2% according to the measure defined in
eq.

S1.9
. This approximate symmetry is inherited from the monolayer
\Gamma
-valley states discussed above. Therefore, we can diagnose the topology from the spin Chern number
C_{s}
defined as
C_{s}=\ln\left(-\xi_{\Gamma_{M}}\xi_{K_{M}}\xi_{K_{M}^{\prime}}\right)/\left(i\frac{2\pi}{3}\right)\bmod 3
with
C_{3z}
eigenvalues
\xi
at
C_{3z}
-invariant momenta
Fang et al. 2012
. As summarized in
figs.

2(a)
,
2(b)
and
2(c)
, all isolated bands in these materials carry a nonzero spin Chern number, realizing either a quantum spin Hall state
\lvert C_{s}\rvert=1
or a high spin Chern state
\lvert C_{s}\rvert=2
as proved later by Wilson loop calculations. The band structures at the other angles can be found in
section

I.2
.

To gain a deeper understanding, we construct continuum models
Zhang et al. 2024
;
Călugăru et al. 2025
for twisted AA/AB-stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and AB-stacked
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
(details in
section

II.3
). The general continuum model reads

\mathcal{H}=\sum_{m,n}\int d^{2}r\left[h(\mathbf{r})\right]_{mn}\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}_{m,\mathbf{r}}\hat{\psi}_{n,\mathbf{r}}
,
where
\hat{\psi}^{\dagger}_{m,\mathbf{r}}
creates an electron at
\mathbf{r}
, and the composite index
m\equiv(l,\alpha,s)
denotes the layer
l
, orbital
\alpha
, and spin
s
. Based on the monolayer IRREP analysis, the basis for
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
includes both
s
-like molecular orbitals with
J_{z}=\pm\frac{1}{2}
and
p
-like molecular orbitals with
J_{z}=\pm\frac{3}{2}
, whereas for
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, we keep only the
p
-like molecular orbital since the
s
-like state lies about
270\text{\,}\mathrm{m}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}
below it due to the large SOC splitting as represented in
fig.

1(c)
. As shown in
figs.

2(a)
,
2(b)
and
2(c)
, the continuum model dispersions agree excellently with DFT and achieve high wavefunction overlap for isolated band sets along high-symmetry lines (
>99\%
in
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
>93\%
for
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
), and the overlap is defined in
eq.

S2.74
. Due to the presence of approximate spin-
\mathrm{U}(1)
symmetry, we calculated the spin-projected Wilson loops in
figs.

2(d)
,
2(e)
and
2(f)
, which confirm that the topmost isolated band set in each system realizes a QSH state. Furthermore, in AA/AB-
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, the subsequent two isolated sets carry a high spin Chern number
\lvert C_{s}\rvert=2
as shown in
fig.

S13
. To gain analytic insight, we simplify the continuum models while retaining low-energy accuracy.
The intralayer part of the simplified Hamiltonian reads,

\displaystyle{}_{ls;ls}^{\mathrm{SnSe}_{2}}=

\displaystyle-\frac{\hbar^{2}\nabla^{2}}{2m_{1}^{*}}+2V_{1}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\cos(\vec{g}_j\cdot\vec{r})

(1)

\displaystyle-2lV_{2}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\sin(\vec{g}_j\cdot\vec{r})+2V_{3}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\cos{(\vec{g}_{2j}\cdot\vec{r})},

\displaystyle[h(\mathbf{r})]_{ls;ls}^{\mathrm{ZrS}_{2}}=

\displaystyle-\frac{\hbar^{2}\nabla^{2}}{2m_{1}^{*}}\sigma_{0}-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m_{2}^{*}}\left(\partial_{+}^{\,2}\,\sigma_{s}^{-}+\partial_{-}^{\,2}\,\sigma_{s}^{+}\right)+V_{1}\sigma_{z}

\displaystyle+2lV_{2}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\Big[e^{i\phi_{j}}\sigma_{s}^{-}+e^{-i\phi_{j}}\sigma_{s}^{+}\Big]\cos\!\big(\mathbf{g}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{r}+\phi_{1}\big),

where
\partial_{\pm}=\partial_{x}\pm i\partial_{y}
,
\sigma_{s}^{\pm}=\tfrac{1}{2}(\sigma_{x}\pm is\sigma_{y})
,
\phi_{j}=\tfrac{2\pi j}{3}-\tfrac{\pi}{6}
. Here
l=\pm 1
denotes the top/bottom layer,
s=\pm 1
denotes spin, and
\sigma_{i}
are Pauli matrices acting on the orbital subspace spanned by
s
-like and
p
-like molecular orbitals. For
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, only the
p
-like molecular orbital is retained, so the orbital index is omitted.
The interlayer coupling terms are given by,

\displaystyle{}_{ls;-ls}^{\mathrm{SnSe}_{2}}=

\displaystyle-\frac{\hbar^{2}\nabla^{2}}{2m_{2}^{*}}+w_{1}+2w_{2}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\cos(\vec{g}_j\cdot\vec{r})

(2)

\displaystyle+2w_{3}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\cos(\vec{g}_{2j}\cdot\vec{r})-2ilsw_{4}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\sin(\vec{g}_{2j}\cdot\vec{r}),

\displaystyle[h(\mathbf{r})]_{ls;-ls}^{\mathrm{ZrS}_{2}}=

\displaystyle w_{1}\sigma_{0}+2w_{2}\sigma_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\cos(\vec{g}_{j}\cdot\vec{r}+\phi_2).

Parameter values are listed in
tables

S4
and
S5
. These reduced models make the emergent approximate symmetries transparent. Twisted AB
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
has an additional inversion
\mathcal{I}
in the simplified continuum model, which is a zero-twist symmetry
Călugăru et al. 2025
and originates from
\mathcal{M}_{z}
of the untwisted AB-stacked bilayer as proved in
section

II.2.1
. For twisted AB-
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, the zero-twist symmetry remains inversion-like in its action on moiré coordinates, but its representation in the orbital subspace is no longer that of a pure inversion. Because the retained
s
-like and
p
-like orbitals have the same inversion parity, a pure inversion would act trivially, whereas they are distinguished through different
J_{z}
. The action on orbital subspace of zero-twist symmetry is therefore equivalent, up to an overall phase, to that of
C_{2z}
, so the simplified model of twisted AB-
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
exhibits emergent
C_{2z}
instead of
\mathcal{I}
. Besides, the simplified Hamiltonians for twisted AA- and AB-stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
are related by the fractional translation
T_{\bm{\tau}_{0}}=\{E|\bm{\tau}_{0}\}
with
\bm{\tau}_{0}=\tfrac{2}{3}\mathbf{a}_{M,1}+\tfrac{1}{3}\mathbf{a}_{M,2}
where
a_{M,1}
and
a_{M,2}
are moiré lattice vectors. This relation is clearly reflected in the LDOS patterns shown in
figs.

2(g)
and
2(h)
and leads to a corresponding phase shift of the
C_{3z}
eigenvalues at
K_{M}/K^{\prime}_{M}
, as discussed in
section

III.3.2
. The origin of
T_{\bm{\tau}_{0}}=\{E|\bm{\tau}_{0}\}
can be understood from the interlayer-distance patterns of relaxed twisted structure at
3.89\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}
. Strictly speaking, the AA and AB corrugation fields cannot be exactly related by a moiré translation, since the two stackings have different symmetries. However, if we quantify the similarity between the two corrugation patterns by the Pearson correlation coefficient defined in
eq.

S2.85
, which is computed between the relaxed interlayer-distance field in AB-case
d_{\mathrm{AB}}(\mathbf{r})
and AA-case
d_{\mathrm{AA}}(\mathbf{r}+\bm{\tau}_{0})
, we obtain
\rho=0.93
. This value shows that the two corrugation fields are close to being
T_{\bm{\tau}_{0}}
-related in the first-harmonic simplified model, and in turn motivates the emergent approximate symmetry
\tilde{C}_{2z}=\{C_{2z}|\bm{\tau}_{0}\}
in twisted AA
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
.
These emergent symmetries,
\mathcal{I}
and
C_{2z}/\tilde{C}_{2z}
, in addition to the spin-
\mathrm{U}(1)
symmetry, account for the near-double degeneracy observed in the low-energy moiré bands of twisted AB-stacked
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and AA/AB-stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
in
figs.

2(a)
,
2(b)
and
2(c)
.

III
Engineering topological flat band in
\Gamma
-valley

Triple-
\mathbf{Q}
description of isolated moiré bands in spin-
\mathrm{U}(1)
-symmetric
\Gamma
-valley systems. Green solid and purple dashed curves denote spin-up and spin-down bands, respectively, and the
C_{3z}
eigenvalues annotated next to top bands are color-matched to their spin sector.
m_{J}
is the angular momentum of the spinful orbital, and
\Pi_{z}\in\{0,\pm 1\}
is the
\mathbf{Q}
-lattice angular momentum. (a) Moiré Brillouin zone with the three nearest
\mathbf{Q}
-sites
\mathbf{Q}^{(j)}
around
K_{M}
and
\mathbf{Q}^{\prime(j)}=-\mathbf{Q}^{(j)}
around
K^{\prime}_{M}
. (b) Schematic band structure of layer group
p3
in the
single-orbital-single-branch
limit, and the constraint
eq.

3
pins the topmost isolated Kramers pair to
C_{s}=0\mod 3
. (c) Schematic band structure of layer group
p321
. Left: in the
single-orbital-single-branch
limit, the upper manifold is a fourfold Dirac-like multiplet at
K_{M}/K^{\prime}_{M}
protected by
C_{2y}
together with the constraint in
eq.

3
, realizing a connected 2D EBR. Right: once an additional orbital or layer-hybridized branch is included, the constraint in
eq.

3
is violated and the multiplet splits into two spin-degenerate doublets. The isolated upper doublet carries
|C_{s}|=1\mod 3
. (d) Schematic band structure of layer group
p312
in both
single-orbital-two-branch
and
two-orbital-single-branch
cases. Left (trivial):
\Pi_{z,\uparrow}^{\mathrm{top}}=\Pi_{z,\downarrow}^{\mathrm{top}}
gives
C_{\uparrow}=0\mod 3
. Right (topological): tuning the relative branch splitting drives a band inversion between blocks with different
\Pi_{z}
in one spin sector, so
\Pi_{z,\uparrow}^{\mathrm{top}}\neq\Pi_{z,\downarrow}^{\mathrm{top}}
and
|C_{s}|=1\mod 3

Building on the results for twisted bilayer
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, we now investigate the microscopic origin of their band topology and derive the condition for isolated topological bands in spin-
\mathrm{U}(1)
-symmetric
\Gamma
-valley moiré systems. Closely related symmetry-based analyses were developed in Ref.
Liu et al. 2025
;
Crépel and Cano 2025
, where the topology of the low-energy moiré bands is inferred from the monolayer symmetry group, the monolayer IRREP at
\Gamma
, and the moiré symmetry group. For the moiré systems with
P31m
or
P3m1
symmetry group that is constructed by monolayer system with
C_{3z}
symmetry, an isolated topological insulating phase is symmetry-enforced by the band representation when the relevant monolayer
\Gamma
state transforms as
\bar{\Gamma}_{4}\bar{\Gamma}_{5}
. Nonetheless, due to the absence of symmetry indicator in the moiré symmetry group, the band representation analysis is not able to distinguish fragile from stable topology. In our case, the approximate spin-
\mathrm{U}(1)
symmetry inherited from the monolayer allows us to distinguish the stable topology from the fragile topology through the
C_{3z}
symmetry indicator
Fang et al. 2012
. Besides, the spin-
\mathrm{U}(1)
symmetry forbids the isolated moiré bands to have stable topology in the
single-orbital-single-branch
limit as shown below.

We begin with the simplest
single-orbital-single-branch
limit with
C_{3}
symmetry, in which the low-energy Hilbert space is described by a single effective orbital with total angular momentum
J_{z}=\pm m_{J}
and a single interlayer-hybridized branch (either layer bonding or antibonding).
At the moiré
\Gamma_{M}
point, we assume the moiré potential is not strong enough to induce level crossings. Thus the topmost Kramers pair is adiabatically connected to the topmost Kramers pair of the untwisted multilayer at
\Gamma
, and its
C_{3z}
eigenvalues are
\xi_{\Gamma_{M},s}=e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}\sigma m_{J}}
with
\sigma=+1
for
s=\uparrow
and
\sigma=-1
for
s=\downarrow
.
To determine
C_{s}
, we next analyze the moiré
K_{M}
and
K^{\prime}_{M}
points. In the continuum description, a basis state at fixed moiré crystal momentum
\mathbf{k}
is denoted by
\ket{\vk,\vQ,s}
, where
\mathbf{Q}
is a moiré reciprocal-lattice vector and the physical momentum is
\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q}
. In the
single-orbital-single-branch
limit, the six spinful low-energy states at
K_{M}
are described by the three basis states,
\ket{K_M,\vQ^{(j)},s}
, where
j=1,2,3
,
\mathbf{Q}^{(1)}=\mathbf{b}_{M,1}
,
\mathbf{Q}^{(2)}=\mathbf{b}_{M,2}
, and
\mathbf{Q}^{(3)}=\mathbf{0}
. Similarly, the corresponding manifold at
\mathbf{k}=K^{\prime}_{M}
is spanned by
\ket{K'_M,\vQ'^{(j)},s}
with
\mathbf{Q}^{\prime(j)}=-\mathbf{Q}^{(j)}
, as shown in
fig.

3(a)
. When the moiré potential is turned off, the three basis states at
K_{M}
and
K^{\prime}_{M}
are related by
C_{3z}
and have the same energy within a fixed spin sector, which therefore form a threefold-degenerate state at
K_{M}
and
K^{\prime}_{M}
. Turning on the moiré potential generically splits each such degenerate state into three nondegenerate eigenstates carrying
\mathbf{Q}
-lattice angular momentum
\Pi_{z}\in\{0,\pm 1\}
and
C_{3z}
eigenvalue
\xi_{K_{M}/K^{\prime}_{M},s}=e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}(\sigma m_{J}+\Pi_{z})}
.

Now we use the leading-harmonic approximation, in which the moiré potential
T_{\mathbf{Q},\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}}
is treated as a long-wavelength periodic modulation and depends only on the momentum transfer
\mathbf{Q}-\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}

Bistritzer and MacDonald 2011
;
Angeli and MacDonald 2021
. In the
single-orbital-single-branch
limit, the hopping among the three
\mathbf{Q}
-sites near
K_{M}
is described by a single complex number
t\equiv T_{\mathbf{Q}^{(1)},\mathbf{Q}^{(2)}}
, while the corresponding hopping near
K^{\prime}_{M}
is
t^{*}
,
i.e.
,
T_{\mathbf{Q}^{(i)},\mathbf{Q}^{(i+1)}}=T_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime(i+1)},\mathbf{Q}^{\prime(i)}}=\big(T_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime(i)},\mathbf{Q}^{\prime(i+1)}}\big)^{*}
with
i+1
understood modulo
3
. A detailed proof can be found in
section

III.2.1
. Consequently, the Hamiltonians within the same spin sector at
K_{M}
and
K^{\prime}_{M}
have the same splitting pattern, but the corresponding states carry opposite
\mathbf{Q}
-lattice angular momentum. More explicitly, if the isolated band in spin sector
s
belongs to the
j
-th split state, its
C_{3z}
eigenvalues at
K_{M}
and
K^{\prime}_{M}
are

\xi^{(j)}_{K_{M},s}=e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}(\sigma m_{J}+\Pi_{z,j})}
and
\xi^{(j)}_{K^{\prime}_{M},s}=e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}(\sigma m_{J}-\Pi_{z,j})}
.
Hence

\xi^{(j)}_{K_{M},s}\xi^{(j)}_{K^{\prime}_{M},s}=e^{-i\frac{4\pi}{3}\sigma m_{J}},

(3)

which is independent of the lattice angular momentum
\Pi_{z,j}
. For a spin-resolved band, we obtain
e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}C_{s}}=-\,e^{-i2\pi\sigma m_{J}}
. Since
m_{J}
is always a half-integer for a spinful orbital, one has
e^{-i2\pi\sigma m_{J}}=-1
, and therefore
e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}C_{s}}=1
,
i.e.
,
C_{s}=0\mod 3
. Thus, in the
single-orbital-single-branch
limit, any isolated moiré Kramers pair has a trivial
C_{3}
symmetry indicator, as shown in
fig.

3(b)

The trivial isolated band derived above relies on the
single-orbital-single-branch
limit. Once an additional orbital and/or branch is included, the moiré hopping is no longer a scalar but a matrix in the orbital/branch subspace,

T_{\mathbf{Q}_{1}m_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2}m_{2}}
,
where
m=(\lambda,\alpha)
is a composite index, and
\lambda
and
\alpha
denote the branch and orbital indices, respectively. In the leading-harmonic approximation, for fixed spin sector
s
,

T_{\mathbf{Q}^{(i)}m_{1},\mathbf{Q}^{(i+1)}m_{2}}=T_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime(i+1)}m_{1},\mathbf{Q}^{\prime(i)}m_{2}}=\big(T_{\mathbf{Q}^{\prime(i)}m_{2},\mathbf{Q}^{\prime(i+1)}m_{1}}\big)^{*}
.
Thus the relation between the
K_{M}
and
K^{\prime}_{M}
triples is now a conjugate transpose in the orbital/branch subspace, rather than a scalar complex conjugation. The constraint
eq.

3
is therefore lost once more than one orbital or more than one interlayer-hybridized branch participates in the low-energy sector. The full derivation is given in
section

III.2.1
.

We now consider the two moiré layer groups associated with the two stackings, both of which possess an additional in-plane
C_{2}
symmetry besides
C_{3z}
. For the layer group
p321
corresponding to twisted AB stacking, it has
C_{2y}
with
x
and
y
axes defined in
fig.

1(a)
. As a result, the bands along this line are spin degenerate. Moreover, in the
single-orbital-single-branch
limit, the exact
C_{2y}
symmetry together with the constraint in
eq.

3
forces the topmost six spinful states at
K_{M}
to decompose into one spin-degenerate doublet with

\xi_{K_{M},s}=e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}\sigma m_{J}}
,
and one fourfold Dirac-like multiplet with

\xi_{K_{M},s}=e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}(\sigma m_{J}-1)}\oplus e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}(\sigma m_{J}+1)}
,
as shown in the left panel of
fig.

3(b)
. A detailed analysis is given in
section

III.2.2
. When the moiré potential is maximized on the honeycomb sites, this fourfold Dirac-like multiplet connects to the topmost Kramers pair at
\Gamma_{M}
, as shown in the left panel of
fig.

3(c)
. Because the constraint in
eq.

3
holds only in the
single-orbital-single-branch
limit, once a second orbital or an additional interlayer-hybridized branch is included, the constraint in
eq.

3
is lost, and the fourfold multiplet at
K_{M}
and
K^{\prime}_{M}
splits into two spin-degenerate doublets. Under the exact
C_{2y}
symmetry, the upper isolated doublet obtained from this splitting necessarily carries a nonzero spin Chern number, and therefore realizes a stable topological band, as shown in the right panel of
fig.

3(c)
.

For layer group
p312
corresponding to twisted AA-stacking with additional
C_{2x}
symmetry, the spin splitting along
\Gamma_{M}-K_{M}
exists in the general case unless fine-tuned. Similar to the case with only
C_{3z}
, isolated moiré band in
single-orbital-single-branch
limit is also trivial constrained by
eq.

3
. We need to include multiple orbitals or multiple layer-hybridized branches to break the constraint and obtain isolated band with stable topology. We illustrate the route to realize the stable topology in the
single-orbital-two-branch
case, while the
two-orbital-single-branch
case is analogous and given in
section

III.2.3
.
Assuming that the orbital carries
J_{z}=\pm m_{z}
and the topmost Kramers-pair band is isolated from lower bands, the corresponding
C_{3z}
eigenvalues at
K_{M}
in spin sector
s
have
\xi_{K_{M},s}=e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}(\sigma m_{J}+\Pi_{z,s}^{\text{top}})}
, and

\xi_{\Gamma_{M},s}=e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}\sigma m_{J}}
.
Because
C_{2x}
and
\mathcal{T}
relate
K_{M}
and
K^{\prime}_{M}
while flipping spin, the isolated states satisfy
\xi_{\Gamma_{M},\bar{s}}\xi_{\Gamma_{M},s}=1
, and
\xi_{K^{\prime}_{M},s}\xi_{K_{M},\bar{s}}=1
.
Using
(\xi_{\Gamma_{M},s})^{3}=-1
for a spinful
C_{3z}
eigenvalue, we have
-\,\xi_{\Gamma_{M},s}=\frac{\xi_{\Gamma_{M},\bar{s}}}{\xi_{\Gamma_{M},s}}
.
Substituting this relation into the indicator formula gives

e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}C_{s}}=\frac{\xi_{K_{M},s}\,\xi_{\Gamma_{M},\bar{s}}}{\xi_{K_{M},\bar{s}}\,\xi_{\Gamma_{M},s}}.

(4)

For the spin-up sector, this becomes

\displaystyle e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}C_{\uparrow}}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}(m_{J}+\Pi_{z,\uparrow}^{\text{top}})}e^{+i\frac{2\pi}{3}m_{J}}}{e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}(-m_{J}+\Pi_{z,\downarrow}^{\text{top}})}e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}m_{J}}}

(5)

\displaystyle=e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}(\Pi_{z,\uparrow}^{\text{top}}-\Pi_{z,\downarrow}^{\text{top}})}.

Therefore, we arrive at
C_{\uparrow}\equiv(\Pi_{z,\uparrow}^{\text{top}}-\Pi_{z,\downarrow}^{\text{top}})\mod 3
.
Hence the isolated band pair is topological precisely when the highest
K_{M}
block in the two spin sectors carries different
\Pi_{z}
. A topological transition occurs when, upon varying model parameters, the highest
K_{M}
block in one spin sector exchanges with another block carrying a different
\Pi_{z}
, as shown in
fig.

3(d)
.
In the first-harmonic continuum models, the energies of these blocks are controlled by the competition between splitting resulted from interlayer hybridization and moiré potential. Consequently, pressure or changes of twist angle might modify the relative strength between moiré potential and interlayer coupling. Such tuning can therefore drive the required band inversion and change
C_{s}
by
\pm 1
.

The topological bands found in twisted bilayer
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
can be understood within this framework. In the cases where the interlayer distance is minimized on the honeycomb sites, the
single-orbital-single-branch
limit produces a Dirac-like manifold at the top of the valence spectrum. Inter-orbital or inter-branch couplings then gap and split this connected manifold, producing the isolated topological band pairs observed in the continuum model and DFT calculations.
Because twisted AA-stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
at
3.89\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}
is approximately related to the twisted AB-stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
by
T_{\bm{\tau}_{0}}
, the moiré potential maxima also form a honeycomb lattice, except that they occupy the
1a
Wyckoff position and one of the honeycomb sites, as shown by the LDOS distribution in
fig.

2(g)
. Consequently, the topological bands are again engineered by the splitting induced by inter-orbital coupling.

IV
Hartree-Fock and Exact-Diagonalization Results

Panels (a–f) show HF phase diagrams. White regions denote gapless phases. Red and grey regions denote gapped phases with Chern number
C=1
and
C=0
, respectively. Light shaded regions indicate phases with inter-spin coherence (ISC). In regions with finite ISC marked by dashed outlines, “
Z_{2}
TI” denotes a topological insulator with broken spin-
\mathrm{U}(1)
symmetry, in contrast to the QSH
1
phase which preserves spin-
\mathrm{U}(1)
. Panels (a, d) correspond to AA-stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, (b, e) to AB-stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, and (c, f) to AB-stacked
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
. The top row (a–c) shows
\nu=-1
, and the bottom row (d–f) shows
\nu=-2
. All HF calculations use a
12\times 12
moiré Brillouin-zone mesh, screening length
d_{\mathrm{sc}}=$20\text{\,}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{m}$
, and projection onto the top four valence moiré bands per spin. Panels (g–j) show calculations at fractional fillings for AA-stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
at
\theta=3.89^{\circ}
with
d_{\mathrm{sc}}=
20\text{\,}\mathrm{n}\mathrm{m}
and
\epsilon_{r}=5
. Panels (g,h) display the FCI spread/gap ratio from ED as a function of the kinetic-energy scaling factor
\alpha_{\mathrm{kin}}=-1.0,-0.8,\ldots,0.8,1.0
for (g)
\nu=-1/3
and (h)
\nu=-2/3
. Values of
\alpha_{\mathrm{kin}}
without markers indicate the absence of clear FCI signatures. The ED is restricted to the highest noninteracting valence band within a single spin sector. (i,j) show the effective HF bandwidth [see
eq.

6
] for (i)
\nu=-1/3
and (j)
\nu=-2/3
.

We now analyze the interacting phases of AA- and AB-stacked twisted bilayer ZrS
2
and AB-stacked twisted bilayer SnSe
2
using self-consistent Hartree-Fock and ED.
Experimentally, accessing these hole fillings in the valence moiré bands requires carrier densities of order
{10}^{13}\text{\,}{\mathrm{cm}}^{-2}{10}^{14}\text{\,}{\mathrm{cm}}^{-2}
, which is within reach of ionic gating via the electric double layer it forms at the surface (see
section

I.4
).
We start from the spin-
\mathrm{U}(1)
symmetric continuum model and include density-density interactions described by a dual-gate screened Coulomb potential

V(\mathbf{q})=\tfrac{e^{2}}{2\epsilon_{0}\epsilon_{r}|\mathbf{q}|}\tanh(|\mathbf q|d_{\mathrm{sc}})
,
with dielectric constant
\epsilon_{r}
setting the effective interaction strength. In HF calculations, we project to the top four moiré bands per spin, and freeze all lower remote bands to be filled. We enforce moiré translation symmetry with the possible exception of inter-spin coherence (ISC) at wavevector
\bm{q}
corresponding to spontaneous breaking of
\mathrm{U}(1)
symmetry. We constrain
\bm{q}
to the
C_{3z}
-symmetric momenta
\Gamma_{M},K_{M},K^{\prime}_{M}
. More details of the implementation are given in
appendix

IV
.

fig.

4
summarizes HF phase diagrams as a function of interaction strength
10/\epsilon_{r}
and twist angle
\theta
at integer fillings
\nu
.
For both stackings of twisted bilayer
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, a spin-polarized Chern insulator with
C=1
at
\nu=-1
appears only beyond a critical interaction strength as shown in
figs.

4(a)
and
4(b)
. For weaker interactions, the system remains metallic. The critical interaction strength grows with
\theta
, consistent with the increasing noninteracting bandwidth of the top valence band. In AB-stacked SnSe
2
, the first moiré valence band is narrower than in twisted ZrS
2
, and therefore the insulating
C=1
phase at
\nu=-1
emerges at interaction strengths as weak as
10/\epsilon_{r}=0.4
, as shown in
fig.

4(c)
. Furthermore, at stronger interaction strengths, both
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
undergo a transition from the
C=1
insulator to a
C=0
insulator at
\nu=-1
.
Due to the indirect energy overlap between the first and second single-particle moiré valence band within each spin sector, a finite interaction strength is required to open a gap in both AA- and AB-stacked ZrS
2
at
\nu=-2
, as shown in
figs.

4(d)
and
4(e)
. Nonetheless, the critical interaction strength for obtaining insulating phases is smaller than at
\nu=-1
for the same twist angle. For example, in AB-stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
at
5\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}
, a gap opens only for
10/\varepsilon_{r}>2.0
at
\nu=-1
, whereas it already opens for
10/\varepsilon_{r}>1.2
at
\nu=-2
. Close to the metal-insulator boundary in both AA- and AB-stacked ZrS
2
, the gapped phase is a time-reversal-symmetric topological phase: either a quantum spin Hall state with spin Chern number
C=1
(QSH
1
) or a
Z_{2}
topological insulator with weak ISC. At stronger interactions, the system develops sizable ISC and the resulting insulator has
C=0
for AA stacking or
C=1
for AB stacking. In
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
at
\nu=-2
, we do not observe a stable QSH
1
or
Z_{2}
topological insulator phase for the interaction strengths
10/\epsilon_{r}\geq 0.4
explored here due to strong ISC and band inversion. Nonetheless, in an intermediate twist angle region
$3.2\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}$<\theta<$5.2\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}$
, we observe a
C=1
Chern insulator with sufficient interaction strength as shown in
fig.

4(f)
. We also study the effect of a displacement field
D
on twisted bilayer
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
at
\theta=3.89^{\circ}
as shown in
fig.

S28
. The displacement field tunes a topological transition between
C=1
and
C=0
spin-polarized insulators at
\nu=-1
, and it suppresses the QSH
1
/
Z_{2}
topological phase in favor of a time-reversal-symmetric gapless state at
\nu=-2
. More results can be found in
appendix

IV
.

To assess the potential for fractional Chern insulators (FCIs)
Neupert et al. 2011
;
Sheng et al. 2011
;
Regnault and Bernevig 2011
;
Sun et al. 2011
;
Tang et al. 2011
;
Yang et al. 2012
, we complement HF with exact diagonalization (ED) calculations. We focus on AA-stacked ZrS
2
at
\theta=3.89^{\circ}
and consider
\nu=-1/3
and
\nu=-2/3
restricted to the topmost valence band within one spin sector. Similar results hold for AB-
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and AB-
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
with twist angles ranging from
2\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}
to
6\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}
. In the HF calculation for AA-stacked ZrS
2
at
\theta=4^{\circ}
, we observe that the bandwidth of the unoccupied HF band nearly doubles for the
C=1
state at
\nu=-1
[
fig.

S27
], driven by the Fock self-energy difference between
\Gamma_{M}
and
K_{M}
. To offset the interaction-induced broadening and explore flatter effective bands, we introduce an artificial scaling factor
\alpha_{\mathrm{kin}}
on the non-interacting dispersion,
E(\mathbf{k})\rightarrow\alpha_{\mathrm{kin}}E(\mathbf{k})
, with
\alpha_{\mathrm{kin}}=1
corresponding to the physical band,
\alpha_{\mathrm{kin}}=0
to a perfectly flat band, and
\alpha_{\mathrm{kin}}<0
to an inverted dispersion. We diagnose FCIs by identifying the putative topological ground-state manifold, which consists of the three lowest states with momenta consistent with the FCI momenta
Regnault and Bernevig 2011
;
Bernevig and Regnault 2012
. The spread of the lowest states should be smaller than the gap between the three lowest states and the lowest state not in this manifold. As shown in
figs.

4(g)
and
4(h)
, robust FCI signatures at
\nu=-1/3
and
\nu=-2/3
appear only within a window of negative
\alpha_{\mathrm{kin}}
. To rationalize this, we estimate an “effective” band dispersion,

E_{\mathrm{eff}}(\mathbf{k})=\alpha_{\mathrm{kin}}E(\mathbf{k})+|\nu|\,E^{\mathrm{HF,int}}(\mathbf{k}),

(6)

where
E^{\mathrm{HF,int}}(\mathbf{k})
is the interaction-induced HF contribution for a fully hole-occupied valence band [see
eq.

S4.162
], rescaled by
|\nu|
to mimic fractional fillings. The effective bandwidth extracted from
E_{\mathrm{eff}}(\mathbf{k})
exhibits a minimum as a function of
\alpha_{\mathrm{kin}}
that closely coincides with the FCI region identified by ED as shown in
figs.

4(i)
and
4(j)
. This indicates that, for the physical dispersion (
\alpha_{\mathrm{kin}}=1
), interaction-induced band broadening drives the system away from the optimal flat-band limit, and FCIs are only stabilized when the bare dispersion is inverted to partially compensate the broadening. The appearance of a
C=1
state at
\nu=-1
is consistent with Ref.
Claassen et al. 2022
. However, we do not find an FCI at
\nu=-1/3
without artificially inverting the band dispersion. This difference is likely related to the ordering of the second and third sets of single-particle bands, which depends on the choice of van der Waals correction. A detailed comparison is provided in
section

I.3
.

V
Discussion

By combining large-scale DFT calculations with faithful continuum models, we establish twisted bilayer
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
as concrete
\Gamma
-valley platforms that host isolated topological moiré bands at small twist angles. Our analysis identifies the key ingredients for such bands in spin-
\mathrm{U}(1)
-symmetric
\Gamma
-valley moiré systems. First, the relevant monolayer state near the band edge should transform as a two-dimensional IRREP without SOC, corresponding to effective orbitals with
m_{l}\neq 0
. This reduces the approximate spin symmetry of the low-energy moiré bands from spin-
\mathrm{SU}(2)
to spin-
\mathrm{U}(1)
. Second, the moiré problem must go beyond the
single-orbital-single-branch
limit where isolated topological bands are symmetry-constrained to be trivial. Inter-orbital or inter-branch couplings generate isolated topological bands either by splitting Dirac-like bands in layer group
p321
or by driving band inversions in layer group
p312
, as confirmed by the simplified models for AB-stacked
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and AA/AB-stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
.
Analysis of the simplified models for these materials validates this framework. Moreover, the competition between bandwidth and interaction yields a rich phase diagram. In both
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
systems, Hartree–Fock calculations find spin-polarized Chern insulators at
\nu=-1
, and symmetry-preserving QSH
1
/
Z_{2}
phases at
\nu=-2
, with the ability to tune Chern numbers and close gaps via a displacement field. Exact diagonalization suggests that interaction-induced Fock broadening currently disfavors FCIs. As a route to stabilize FCIs, future work may explore enhancing dielectric screening asymmetrically or leveraging substrate-induced moiré potentials to quench the kinetic energy further, thereby realizing the full potential of these
\Gamma
-valley platforms.

Appendix I
Ab initio
calculations

I.1
Untwist monolayer/bilayer results

((a))

((b))

Figure S5:
The monolayer crystal structure of
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and corresponding band structure. (a) and (b) show monolayer crystal structure of
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
from the top view and side view, respectively. The monolayer band structure with SOC of (c)
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and (d)
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
with
p
-orbital projection of Se and S atoms, respectively. The red and green colors indicate the orbital weight of
p_{x,y}
and
p_{z}
, respectively. The enlarge monolayer band structure of (e)
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and (f)
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
without (black lines) and with (red lines) spin-orbit coupling. We label the double irreducible representation (IRREP) of the two topmost bands at the
\Gamma
point.

In this section, we present
ab initio
results for the monolayer and untwisted bilayer structure of
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
. As shown in
figs.

S5(a)
and
S5(b)
, the monolayer crystal structures of both materials have Shubnikov Space Group (SSG) 164.86
P\bar{3}m11^{\prime}
symmetry, characterized by symmetry generators
C_{3z}
,
C_{2}
with rotated axes along
[100]
direction, inversion symmetry
\mathcal{I}
and time-reversal symmetry
\mathcal{T}
. The hexagonal lattice vectors are given by

\mathbf{a}_{1}=a\left(1,0\right),\quad\mathbf{a}_{2}=a\left(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right),

(S1.7)

where
a
is the lattice constant, and the atoms are located at

\mathrm{Sn/Zr}:\ \left(0,0,0\right),\quad\mathrm{Se/S}:\ \left(\frac{1}{3},\frac{2}{3},z\right),\,\left(\frac{2}{3},\frac{1}{3},-z\right).

(S1.8)

For
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, the lattice constant and out-of-plane displacement of Se or S atoms are
a=$3.811\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolAngstrom}$
,
z=$1.528\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolAngstrom}$

Wu et al. 2019b
and
a=$3.661\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolAngstrom}$
,
z=$1.441\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolAngstrom}$

Al-Alamy et al. 1977
, respectively. The Sn or Zr atom is located at the
1a
Wyckoff position featuring
\bar{3}m
site symmetry, while the Se or S atoms are positioned at
2d
Wyckoff position and have
3m
symmetry. Within a unit cell, the two
2d
Wyckoff positions are mapped to one another by inversion.

We focus on the valence bands since the conduction bands have been fully studied in
Călugăru et al. 2025
. The valence band structures of monolayer
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
with SOC is shown in
figs.

S5(c)
and
S5(d)
. Due to the presence of
\mathcal{PT}
symmetry, every band in both
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
is doubly degenerate across the whole Brillouin Zone (BZ). In both materials, the
\Gamma
valley is dominated by the
(p_{x},p_{y})
orbitals of chalcogenide atoms. A comparison of the low-energy bands without and with SOC is shown in
figs.

S5(e)
and
S5(f)
. Without SOC included, the valence band maxima (VBM) of
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
are located at
\Gamma
while the VBM of
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
is located on
\Gamma-M
path that is near
\Gamma
. As shown by the red lines, SOC lifts the twofold degeneracy (without spin degree of freedom) of the topmost state at
\Gamma
point in both
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
. In
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, SOC further shifts the VBM from a high symmetry line to the
\Gamma
point. Because Se atom is heavier than the S atom, we can find the SOC splitting, defined as the separation between the two spinful Kramers doublets obtained from the spinless twofold-degenerate state (or fourfold with spin) at
\Gamma
, in
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
(
278\text{\,}\mathrm{m}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}
) is much stronger than that in
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
(
83\text{\,}\mathrm{m}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}
). The first and second topmost double-degenerated bands at
\Gamma
in
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
transform as the
\overline{\Gamma}_{6}\overline{\Gamma}_{7}
and
\overline{\Gamma}_{9}
double irreducible representations (IRREPs) in the spinful case, while the first and second topmost double-degenerated bands at
\Gamma
in
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
transform as the
\overline{\Gamma}_{4}\overline{\Gamma}_{5}
and
\overline{\Gamma}_{8}
double IRREPs. Although both monolayers share the same lattice symmetry and have
\Gamma
-valley valence states dominated by chalcogen
(p_{x},p_{y})
orbitals, the corresponding
\Gamma
-point IRREP is not fixed by orbital character alone. Because the two chalcogen atoms in one monolayer unit cell are related by inversion, the
(p_{x},p_{y})
manifold forms even- and odd-parity layer combinations, and the ordering of these two doublets depends on their hybridization with nearby metal-derived states. Here the two compounds differ qualitatively: in
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
the low-energy conduction bands is mainly Zr-
4d
, while in
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
it is mainly Sn/Se-
(s,p)
derived. This difference changes how the even- and odd-parity chalcogen
(p_{x},p_{y})
combinations are renormalized, and hence leads to different parity ordering at
\Gamma
.

((a))

((b))

((c))

((d))

((e))

((f))

Figure S6:
The crystal and band structure for untwisted AA/AB-stacking bilayer SnSe
2
and ZrS
2
. (a) and (d) show the AA- and AB-stacked bilayer crystal structures, respectively. (b) and (e) show the band structure for AA- and AB-stacked untwisted bilayer
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, respectively. (c) and (f) show the band structure for AA- and AB-stacked untwisted bilayer
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, respectively. The black and red colors denote the band structures without and with spin-orbital coupling included.

By stacking one monolayer directly on top of another, we obtain the AA-stacked bilayer as shown in
fig.

S6(a)
. The symmetry group of the AA-stacked bilayer is the same as the monolayer case,
i.e.
, SSG 164.86
P\bar{3}m11^{\prime}
. The band structure for AA-stacked
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
are shown in
figs.

S6(b)
and
S6(c)
.
Because
\mathcal{PT}
symmetry is present, all bands are doubly degenerate throughout the Brillouin zone.
Comparing the monolayer band structures without SOC in
figs.

S5(e)
and
S5(f)
with the bilayer band structures without SOC in
figs.

S6(b)
and
S6(c)
, we find that interlayer coupling splits the topmost monolayer
\Gamma
state into the top two interlayer bonding and antibonding states at bilayer
\Gamma
. The corresponding interlayer splitting, defined as the bonding–antibonding splitting of the topmost
\Gamma
state, is
90\text{\,}\mathrm{m}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}
and
74\text{\,}\mathrm{m}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}
in
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, respectively. As in the monolayer, including SOC shifts the VBM of
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
from a high-symmetry line to
\Gamma
and opens gaps at high-symmetry points/lines. Notably, in
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
the SOC and interlayer energy scales are comparable, making the ordering of topmost 8 spinful states at
\Gamma
sensitive to the choice of van der Waals (vdW) corrections (see detailed discussion in
section

I.3
).

Because the monolayer structure lacks
C_{2z}
symmetry, we can apply
C_{2z}
to the bottom layer of AA-stacked bilayer and obtain the AB-stacked bilayer as seen in
fig.

S6(d)
. The AB-stacked bilayer has
P\bar{6}m21^{\prime}
(SSG 187.210) symmetry which is generated by
C_{3z}
, vertical mirror
M_{100}
, horizontal mirror
M_{z}
and
\mathcal{T}
. The head-to-head configuration of AB-stacking results in larger interlayer distances (
7.016\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolAngstrom}
for
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
6.362\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolAngstrom}
for
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
) compared to the AA-stacking configuration (
6.166\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolAngstrom}
for
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
5.866\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolAngstrom}
for
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
). Here, the interlayer distance is defined as the distance between Sn/Zr atomic sub-layers. Consequently, the interlayer splitting in AB-stacking (
36\text{\,}\mathrm{m}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}
for
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
57\text{\,}\mathrm{m}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}
for
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
) is smaller than that in AA-stacking. Moreover, double degeneracy in AB-stacking is only protected along the
\Gamma-M
line by
M_{010}M_{z}
.

I.2
Twisted AA/AB-stacked bilayer

By rotating the top layer relative to the bottom one, we obtain the AA- and AB-stacked twisted bilayer structures at a series of angles. We take the convention that
\hat{y}
direction aligns with the
[010]
direction as shown in
fig.

S7
, following Ref.
Călugăru et al. 2025
. The twisted AA-stacked bilayer
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
break
\mathcal{I}
symmetry in the untwist configuration and belong to SSG
P3121\textquoteright
(No. 149.22) which is characterized by symmetry generators
C_{3z}
,
C_{2x}
,
\mathcal{T}
, and translational symmetry
T_{\mathbf{R}}
, as shown in
fig.

S7(a)
. The twisted AB-stacked bilayer
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
break
\mathcal{M}_{z}
symmetry in the untwist configuration and belong to SSG
P3211\textquoteright
(No. 150.26) with symmetry generators
C_{3z}
,
C_{2y}
,
\mathcal{T}
, and translational symmetry
T_{\mathbf{R}}
, as shown in
fig.

S7(b)
. We first study the moiré band structures using large-scale DFT calculation (the M-valley conduction bands are detailed in Ref.
Călugăru et al. 2025
). The valence bands for twisted
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
(
fig.

S8
) and
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
(
fig.

S9
) are shown for AA (top rows) and AB (bottom rows) from
9.43\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}
to
3.89\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}
. At
3.89\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}
, the top two spinful bands in AB-
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
form a single isolated set. We don’t discuss the top isolated band in AA-
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
since the direct gap between the second and third spinful band is too small. In AA/AB-
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
at
3.89\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}
, the top six spinful bands organize into three isolated sets of two spinful bands each. Moreover, the bands are nearly doubly degenerate along high-symmetry lines. As shown in
section

II.3
, this degeneracy arises from zero-twist inversion
\mathcal{I}
in twisted AB-stacked structure and from the
\tilde{C}_{2z}
symmetry in twisted AA-
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
.

Moreover, by inspecting the DFT wavefunction along high-symmetry lines, we find that the isolated bands in both
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
remain nearly eigenstates of
s_{z}
, which is the Pauli matrix acting in spin space and trivially on all other degrees of freedom. We define spin-
\mathrm{U}(1)
symmetry as
[H(\mathbf{k}),s_{z}]=0
and quantify its breaking by

\delta_{s}^{(n)}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{1-\big|\langle u_{n\mathbf{k}}|s_{z}|u_{n\mathbf{k}}\rangle\big|}{2}.

(S1.9)

Here
|u_{n\mathbf{k}}\rangle
denotes the eigenstate of the
n
-th band at momentum
\mathbf{k}
, and
We find
\delta_{s}^{(n)}(\mathbf{k})\leq 2\%
for the isolated bands of both
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
. We will verify this again using the continuum model in
section

II.3
.

Therefore we could define a spin Chern number
C_{s}
of each spin sector for each isolated band set,

C_{s}=\ln\left(-\xi_{\Gamma_{M}}\xi_{K_{M}}\xi_{K_{M}^{\prime}}\right)/\left(i\frac{2\pi}{3}\right)\bmod 3,

(S1.10)

where
\xi_{\mathbf{k}}
is the
C_{3z}
eigenvalue at
\mathbf{k}\in\{\Gamma_{M},K_{M},K^{\prime}_{M}\}
. Evaluating these eigenvalues for
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, we summarize the resulting spin Chern numbers in
tables

S3
and
S2
, where all the isolated bands in
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and AB-stacked
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
carry a nontrivial spin Chern number.

((b))
