I.3
Influence of vdW correction

In
section

I.1
, we mentioned that the energy scale of interlayer splitting (
74\text{\,}\mathrm{m}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}
in AA-stacking and
57\text{\,}\mathrm{m}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}
in AB-stacking) and SOC energy splitting (
83\text{\,}\mathrm{m}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}
) is comparable in
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
. Because the interlayer coupling strength is strongly related to the interlayer distance, the band structures of both untwisted and twisted bilayer
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
are sensitive to the choice of vdW. To illustrate this, we relaxed untwisted AA–
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
using Grimme DFT-D3 (D3-vdW) and Tkatchenko–Scheffler (TS-vdW) corrections. Bilayer structure relaxed by D3-vdW has a larger interlayer distance
5.866\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolAngstrom}
than the one relaxed by TS-vdW
5.683\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolAngstrom}
. Consequently, TS-relaxed structure shows a larger splitting (
105\text{\,}\mathrm{m}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}
) than the D3-relaxed one (
74\text{\,}\mathrm{m}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}
) due to the smaller interlayer distance as shown in
fig.

S10(a)
. Moreover, the interlayer splitting is slightly larger than the SOC splitting, whereas in the TS-relaxed structure, the SOC splitting is slightly larger than the interlayer splitting. Consequently, the inclusion of SOC leads to a different ordering of layer hybridized antibonding and bonding states at
\Gamma
. Specifically, in the TS-relaxed structure, the top two states at the
\Gamma
point are both layer-hybridized antibonding states (
\bar{\Gamma}_{6}\bar{\Gamma}_{7}
and
\bar{\Gamma}_{9}
) as shown in
fig.

S10(b)
. These two states arise from the interlayer hybridization and subsequent splitting of the corresponding monolayer
\bar{\Gamma}_{6}\bar{\Gamma}_{7}
and
\bar{\Gamma}_{9}
states (
fig.

S5(f)
). In contrast, in the D3-relaxed structure, the top two states are antibonding and bonding states (
\bar{\Gamma}_{6}\bar{\Gamma}_{7}
and
\bar{\Gamma}_{4}\bar{\Gamma}_{5}
, respectively as shown in
fig.

S10(b)
) that originates solely from the interlayer-hybridization-induced splitting of the monolayer
\bar{\Gamma}_{6}\bar{\Gamma}_{7}
state. The band order swap is corroborated by the symmetry representations at
\Gamma
, as shown in
fig.

S10(b)
. The different ordering is inherited by the twisted bilayer, as shown in
fig.

S9
(relaxed by D3-vdW) and in Ref.
Claassen et al. 2022
(relaxed by TS-vdW).

((a))

((b))

Figure S10:
Comparison of band structures for untwisted AA-stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
bilayer with D3-vdW and TS-vdW corrections, (a) without and (b) with SOC. In (b), the irreducible representations (IRREPs) of the top four bands are labeled, with
\pm
denoting their corresponding parity. We observe that the middle two bands are switched under the two vdW corrections.

I.4
Ionic Gating of Two-Dimensional Semiconductors

For many two-dimensional semiconductors to be experimentally useful, it is crucial to gate them efficiently from their intrinsic semiconducting gap into the conduction or valence band, so that measurable electron or hole transport can be achieved. Ionic gating is particularly well suited for this task because it forms an electric double layer at the surface, acting like an ultra-thin capacitor with a very large capacitance (typically
\sim
1\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolMicro F}\text{\,}{\mathrm{cm}}^{-2}50\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolMicro F}\text{\,}{\mathrm{cm}}^{-2}
), far exceeding that of conventional oxide gates. This allows carrier density changes on the order of
{10}^{13}\text{\,}{\mathrm{cm}}^{-2}{10}^{14}\text{\,}{\mathrm{cm}}^{-2}
, sufficient to move the Fermi level by
\sim
0.4\text{\,}\mathrm{eV}0.8\text{\,}\mathrm{eV}
and drive the system from the gap deep into either the electron or hole bands. Such densities are high enough not only to enable ambipolar transport in materials like transition metal dichalcogenides, but in some cases to induce metallic or even superconducting behavior. Depending on the application, ionic gating can be implemented using liquid ionic electrolytes, ion gels, polymer electrolytes, or solid-state ionic conductors, offering a versatile platform for strong and reversible electrostatic doping of 2D crystals.

Ionic gating has been demonstrated to induce the required carrier densities in closely related 2D materials, including monolayer and few-layer transition metal dichalcogenides
Ye et al. 2012
;
Zhang et al. 2012
;
Saito et al. 2015
and twisted bilayer graphene-based moiré systems. At low temperatures, the ionic liquid is frozen and the induced charge is fixed, making ionic gating compatible with the transport and spectroscopic probes needed to resolve the Chern-insulator and QSH phases predicted in
section

IV
. Combined with a conventional back gate, ionic gating also allows independent tuning of the carrier density and the out-of-plane displacement field, enabling the displacement-field-driven topological transitions discussed in
fig.

S28
.

Appendix II
Continuum model

To elucidate the topology and related physics in twisted bilayer
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, we construct continuum models following Refs.
Călugăru et al. 2025
;
Zhang et al. 2024
. We briefly summarize the method here, and full details can be found in those references. We first perform Wannier and valley projection of the DFT Hamiltonian to retain only the target orbitals and valleys, yielding a reduced DFT Hamiltonian. We then parameterize a symmetry-constrained continuum model and determine the coefficients of the symmetry-allowed terms by linear least-squares fitting to the reduced Hamiltonian, as in Refs.
Călugăru et al. 2025
;
Zhang et al. 2024
. The obtained model reproduces the low-energy band structure and wavefunctions with numerical accuracy. Nonetheless, this model can still contain many terms that hinder analytical understanding. Therefore, we further simplify the model by combining the nonlinear fitting with the step-wise regression procedure proposed in Ref.
Călugăru et al. 2025
. Finally, we can obtain a model with far fewer parameters that still captures the low-energy physics quantitatively.

II.1
Reduced DFT Hamiltonian obtained from projection method

In this section, we summarize the DFT calculation and the projection setup used to obtain the reduced DFT Hamiltonian, following the notation of Ref.
Călugăru et al. 2025
. The full Kohn-Sham Hamiltonian is obtained from the OpenMX
Ozaki 2003
;
Ozaki and Kino 2004
package, which is expressed in the non-orthonormal pseudo-atomic orbital (PAO) basis. We denote the PAO basis as
\ket{\varphi_{il\alpha s} \left( \vR_M + \vec{\tau}_{il\alpha} \right)}
, where
\mathbf{R}_{M}
denotes moiré cell,
l
labels the layer,
i
indexes the monolayer unit cell within the moiré supercell,
\alpha
labels the orbital, and
s
is the spin. For
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, we use Zr-
s2p2d2
and S-
s2p2d2
PAO basis set. For
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, we use Sn-
s2p2d2f1
and Se-
s2p2d2
PAO basis set.

The Wannier projection procedure is inspired by the construction of Wannier functions
Löwdin 1950
;
Cloizeaux 1964
;
Marzari and Vanderbilt 1997
;
Marzari et al. 2012
. In this step, our goal is to construct a set of orthonormal Wannier bases that faithfully spans the isolated low-energy subspace. To this end, we first analyze the orbital component of the isolated low-energy valence bands. As shown in
figs.

S5(c)
and
S5(d)
, the top 12 valence bands are separated from the rest and dominated by
p
-orbitals of chalcogen atoms in both
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
. Accordingly, for each monolayer unit cell we choose three
p
-orbitals on the two S/Se sites as the projected trial states
\ket{\phi_{il\alpha s}\left(\vR_M + \vec{\tau}_{il\alpha} \right)}
, which gives
1\leq\alpha\leq 6
. We then project these trial states onto the isolated low-energy subspace
\mathcal{A}
, spanned by the Kohn-Sham eigenstates
\{\ket{\psi_n}\}_{n\in\mathcal{A}}
, through the projector
\hat{P}_{\mathcal{A}}=\sum_{n\in\mathcal{A}}\ket{\psi_n}\bra{\psi_n}
.
The projected trial states are thus defined as
\ket{\bar{\phi}_{i l \alpha s}(\vR_M+\vec{\tau}_{i l \alpha})}=\hat{P}_{\mathcal{A}}\ket{\phi_{i l \alpha s}(\vR_M+\vec{\tau}_{i l \alpha})}
. Since these projected states are generally not orthonormal, we introduce their overlap matrix
\mathcal{S}
, whose matrix elements are
\mathcal{S}_{il\alpha s;jl^{\prime}\beta s^{\prime}}=\bra{\bar{\phi}_{i l \alpha s}(\vR_M+\vec{\tau}_{i l \alpha})}\ket{\bar{\phi}_{j l' \beta s'}(\vR_M+\vec{\tau}_{j l' \beta})}
. The refined trial basis is then obtained by Löwdin orthonormalization
Călugăru et al. 2025
;
Löwdin 1950
;
Cloizeaux 1964
;
Marzari and Vanderbilt 1997
;
Marzari et al. 2012
as
\ket{\tilde{\phi}_{i l \alpha s}(\vR_M+\vec{\tau}_{i l \alpha})}=\sum_{j,l^{\prime},\beta,s^{\prime}}\ket{\bar{\phi}_{j l' \beta s'}(\vR_M+\vec{\tau}_{j l' \beta})}\left(\mathcal{S}^{-1/2}\right)_{jl^{\prime}\beta s^{\prime};il\alpha s}
,
where
\mathcal{S}^{-1/2}
is the inverse square root of the overlap matrix. By construction, the states
\ket{\tilde{\phi}_{i l \alpha s}(\vR_M+\vec{\tau}_{i l \alpha})}
are orthonormal and exactly span the same isolated low-energy subspace
\mathcal{A}
, while remaining adiabatically connected to the original trial orbitals.

((a))

((b))

((c))

Figure S11:
The construction of the molecular basis corresponding to the topmost two monolayer states at
\Gamma
in
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
.

After the Wannier projection, we obtain a reduced Hamiltonian in the orthonormal refined trial basis
\ket{\tilde{\phi}_{i,l,\alpha,s}\left(\vR_M + \vec{\tau}_{il\alpha} \right)}
. Compared with the original DFT Hamiltonian, the Wannier-projected Hamiltonian is reduced to dimension
N_{\text{layer}}\times N_{a}\times N_{s}\times N_{\text{trial}}=24N_{a}
with
N_{a}
the number of monolayer unit cells per moiré cell, which greatly simplifies the subsequent steps. The Wannier-projected Hamiltonian is further reduced by a valley projection, analogous to the truncated atomic plane-wave (TAPW) method
Miao et al. 2023
but with additional Löwdin orthogonalization procedure. We then construct trial states to further project Wannier-projected Hamiltonian into
\Gamma
-valley, and the trial states are taken as atomic plane-wave combinations of the refined projected basis in the Wannier projection procedure,

\displaystyle\ket{\phi_{ l\alpha s} \left( \vk, \vQ_l\right)}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{NN_{a}}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}_{M}}\sum_{i}e^{i\left(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{G}_{M}\right)\cdot\left(\mathbf{R}_{M}+{\tau}_{il\alpha}\right)}\ket{\tilde{\phi}_{i l \alpha s} \left( \vR_M + \vec{\tau}_{il\alpha} \right)}

(S2.11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{NN_{a}}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}_{M}}\sum_{i}e^{i\left(\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{\Gamma}_{l}-\mathbf{Q}_{l}\right)\cdot\left(\mathbf{R}_{M}+{\tau}_{il\alpha}\right)}\ket{\tilde{\phi}_{i l \alpha s} \left( \vR_M + \vec{\tau}_{il\alpha} \right)},

where
N
is the number of moiré unit cell,
\mathbf{k}
lies in the first moiré Brillouin zone, and
\mathbf{G}_{M}=n_{1}\mathbf{b}_{M,1}+n_{2}\mathbf{b}_{M,2}
with
n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
denotes a moiré reciprocal lattice vector. Since the low-energy moiré physics is related to states near a monolayer
\Gamma
-valley, we write
\mathbf{G}_{M}=\mathbf{\Gamma}_{l}-\mathbf{Q}_{l}
(
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{l}
is the
\Gamma
momenta for layer
l
) and retain only a subset of
\mathbf{Q}_{l}
vectors nearest to
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{l}
.
Moreover, because the low-energy moiré bands are dominated by the lowest
\Gamma
-valley states of the monolayer configurations, the Hilbert space spanned by the chalcogen
p
-orbitals can be further reduced. Specifically, in monolayer
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, the two highest doubly degenerate spinful states at
\Gamma
are separated from the lower valence bands by
0.73\text{\,}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}
. Their corresponding double-group IRREPs are
\overline{\Gamma}_{6}\overline{\Gamma}_{7}
and
\overline{\Gamma}_{9}
, with out-of-plane angular momentum
J_{z}=\pm\frac{3}{2}
and
J_{z}=\pm\frac{1}{2}
, respectively, as shown in
fig.

S5(f)
. Since these states are dominated by the S-
(p_{x},p_{y})
orbitals [
fig.

S5(d)
], their orbital basis can be written approximately as

\displaystyle\overline{\Gamma}_{6}\overline{\Gamma}_{7}

\displaystyle:\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\ket{p_{+},\uparrow,\mathrm{S1}}+\ket{p_{+},\uparrow,\mathrm{S2}}\right),\ \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\ket{p_{-},\downarrow,\mathrm{S1}}+\ket{p_{-},\downarrow,\mathrm{S2}}\right)\right\},

(S2.12)

\displaystyle\overline{\Gamma}_{9}

\displaystyle:\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\ket{p_{+},\downarrow,\mathrm{S1}}+\ket{p_{+},\downarrow,\mathrm{S2}}\right),\ \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\ket{p_{-},\uparrow,\mathrm{S1}}+\ket{p_{-},\uparrow,\mathrm{S2}}\right)\right\},

where
p_{\pm}=(p_{x}\pm ip_{y})/\sqrt{2}
,
\uparrow
and
\downarrow
denote spin up and spin down, and
\mathrm{S1}
and
\mathrm{S2}
label the two chalcogen atoms within a monolayer unit cell with fractional coordinates
\left(\frac{1}{3},\frac{2}{3}\right)
and
\left(\frac{2}{3},\frac{1}{3}\right)
, respectively. Symmetry wise, the basis is equivalent to placing a spinful molecular orbital on
1a

(0,0)
Wyckoff position with
J_{z}=\pm\frac{3}{2}
and
J_{z}=\pm\frac{1}{2}
for
\overline{\Gamma}_{6}\overline{\Gamma}_{7}
and
\overline{\Gamma}_{9}
, respectively. For brevity, we denote the spinful molecular basis of the
\overline{\Gamma}_{6}\overline{\Gamma}_{7}
manifold as
\ket{P_{\pm}}
, and the one of the
\overline{\Gamma}_{9}
manifold as
\ket{\bar{s}_\pm}
. Here,
\bar{s}
indicates a molecular orbital with odd parity under inversion, distinguishing it from an even-parity atomic
s
orbital.
Similarly, for
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, the highest two doubly degenerate spinful states transform according to
\overline{\Gamma}_{4}\overline{\Gamma}_{5}
and
\overline{\Gamma}_{8}
, respectively, as shown in
fig.

S5(e)
. Note that these two IRREPs have even parity under inversion, which is different from the
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
case. Accordingly, the orbital basis for these two states are

\displaystyle\overline{\Gamma}_{4}\overline{\Gamma}_{5}

\displaystyle:\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\ket{p_{+},\uparrow,\mathrm{Se1}}-\ket{p_{+},\uparrow,\mathrm{Se2}}\right),\ \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\ket{p_{-},\downarrow,\mathrm{Se1}}-\ket{p_{-},\downarrow,\mathrm{Se2}}\right)\right\},

(S2.13)

\displaystyle\overline{\Gamma}_{8}

\displaystyle:\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\ket{p_{+},\downarrow,\mathrm{Se1}}-\ket{p_{+},\downarrow,\mathrm{Se2}}\right),\ \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\ket{p_{-},\uparrow,\mathrm{Se1}}-\ket{p_{-},\uparrow,\mathrm{Se2}}\right)\right\},

\mathrm{Se1}
and
\mathrm{Se2}
label the two chalcogen atoms within a monolayer unit cell at fractional coordinates
\left(\frac{1}{3},\frac{2}{3}\right)
and
\left(\frac{2}{3},\frac{1}{3}\right)
, respectively. Symmetry wise, the basis is equivalent to placing a spinful molecular orbital on
1a

(0,0)
Wyckoff position with
J_{z}=\pm\frac{3}{2}
and
J_{z}=\pm\frac{1}{2}
for
\overline{\Gamma}_{4}\overline{\Gamma}_{5}
and
\overline{\Gamma}_{8}
, respectively, as shown in
fig.

S11
. For brevity, we denote the molecular basis of the
\overline{\Gamma}_{4}\overline{\Gamma}_{5}
manifold by
\ket{R_\pm}
, and the one of the
\overline{\Gamma}_{8}
manifold by
\ket{s_\pm}
. Here,
R
denotes a molecular orbital with even parity under inversion, distinguishing it from the odd-parity molecular orbital
P
introduced in the
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
case. In practice, we retain only the highest doubly degenerate
\overline{\Gamma}_{4}\overline{\Gamma}_{5}
band at
\Gamma
as the continuum-model basis, since it is separated from the lower bands by
0.27\text{\,}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}
, as shown in
fig.

S5(e)
.

After the valley projection and the secondary Wannier projection, we obtain a projected Kohn-Sham Hamiltonian in the plane-wave basis that faithfully reproduces the low-energy
\Gamma
-valley moiré Hamiltonian, while greatly reducing the Hilbert-space dimension,

\left[h^{\mathrm{DFT}}_{\mathbf{Q},\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})\right]_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};\,l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}=\bra{\phi_{l_1\alpha_1 s_1}(\vk,\vQ)}\hat{H}_{\mathrm{KS}}\ket{\phi_{l_2\alpha_2 s_2}(\vk,\vQ')}.

(S2.14)

Here, we suppress the layer index on
\mathbf{Q}
, since the two layers share the same
\mathbf{Q}
-mesh in the
\Gamma
-valley moiré system. The index
\alpha
labels the retained
\Gamma
-valley orbital channels:
\alpha\in\{P,\bar{s}\}
for
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, and
\alpha=R
for
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
.

II.2
General continuum model constrained by symmetry

In this section, we derive an explicit continuum Hamiltonian that produces the reduced DFT Hamiltonian
\left[h^{\mathrm{DFT}}_{\mathbf{Q},\mathbf{Q}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{k})\right]_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};\,l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}
on a chosen set of momenta and can subsequently be evaluated at arbitrary
\mathbf{k}
without repeating the projection procedures. To this end, we first write down a general real-space continuum Hamiltonian in terms of the continuum field operators
\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l,\alpha,s}(\mathbf{r})
following Ref.
Zhang et al. 2024
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n_{x},n_{y}\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1}\\
l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}\end{subarray}}\int\differential[2]{r}t^{n_{x},n_{y}}_{l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1};l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}\left(\mathbf{r}\right)\left[i^{n_{x}+n_{y}}\left(\partial^{n_{x}}_{x}\partial^{n_{y}}_{y}\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1}}\left(\mathbf{r}\right)\right)\hat{c}_{l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}\left(\mathbf{r}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+(-i)^{n_{x}+n_{y}}\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1}}\left(\mathbf{r}\right)\partial^{n_{x}}_{x}\partial^{n_{y}}_{y}\hat{c}_{l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}\left(\mathbf{r}\right)\right],

Here,
l
,
\alpha
and
s
label the layer, orbital and spin, respectively. As in the reduced DFT Hamiltonian,
\alpha\in\{P,\bar{s}\}
for
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, while
\alpha\in\{R\}
for
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
. The coefficient
t^{n_{x},n_{y}}_{l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1};\,l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}(\mathbf{r})
denotes the generalized moiré potential and satisfies the Hermiticity condition

t^{n_{x},n_{y}}_{l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1};l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}\left(\mathbf{r}\right)=\left(t^{n_{x},n_{y}}_{l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2};l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1}}\left(\mathbf{r}\right)\right)^{*}.

(S2.15)

Within this general gradient-expansion framework, the usual distinction between the intralayer kinetic term and the moiré potential is no longer explicit. Instead, both are incorporated into the coefficient functions
t^{n_{x},n_{y}}_{l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1};l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}\left(\mathbf{r}\right)
.
Moiré translational symmetry requires

t^{n_{x},n_{y}}_{l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1};l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}\left(\mathbf{r}+\mathbf{R}_{M}\right)=t^{n_{x},n_{y}}_{l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1};l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}\left(\mathbf{r}\right),

(S2.16)

where
\mathbf{R}_{M}=m_{1}\mathbf{a}_{M_{1}}+m_{2}\mathbf{a}_{M_{2}}
with
m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
denotes a moiré lattice vector. Consequently,

t^{n_{x},n_{y}}_{l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1};\,l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}(\mathbf{r})=\sum_{\mathbf{G}_{M}}t^{n_{x},n_{y}}_{l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1};\,l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2};\mathbf{G}_{M}}e^{-i\mathbf{G}_{M}\cdot\mathbf{r}},

(S2.17)

where
\mathbf{G}_{M}=n_{1}\mathbf{b}_{M,1}+n_{2}\mathbf{b}_{M,2}
with
n_{1},n_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
denotes a moiré reciprocal lattice vector.
It is convenient to recast the continuum Hamiltonian in momentum space. For the
\Gamma
-valley problem, the two layers share the same valley center, so we suppress
\Gamma_{l}
in the following discussion. We define

\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},l,\alpha,s}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Omega}}\int d^{2}r\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l,\alpha,s}(\mathbf{r})e^{i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{r}},

(S2.18)

with inverse transform

\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l,\alpha,s}(\mathbf{r})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Omega}}\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathrm{MBZ}}\sum_{\mathbf{Q}}\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},l,\alpha,s}e^{-i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q})\cdot\mathbf{r}},

(S2.19)

where
\Omega
is the total area of the moiré system,
\mathbf{k}
lies in the first moiré Brillouin zone, and
\mathbf{Q}
runs over the chosen plane-wave mesh.
Substituting
eq.

S2.19
and
eq.

S2.17
into
section

II.2
, we can obtain the continuum model in the momentum space,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{x},n_{y}\in\mathbb{Z}\\
\mathbf{k},\mathbf{G}_{M}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{Q}_{i},\mathbf{Q}_{j}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{1},l_{1},\alpha_{1}\\
s_{2},l_{2},\alpha_{2}\end{subarray}}\frac{\left(k_{x}-Q_{i,x}\right)^{n_{x}}\left(k_{y}-Q_{i,y}\right)^{n_{y}}+\left(k_{x}-Q_{j,x}\right)^{n_{x}}\left(k_{y}-Q_{j,y}\right)^{n_{y}}}{2}

(S2.20)

\displaystyle\times t^{n_{x},n_{y}}_{l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1};l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2};\mathbf{G}_{M}}\delta_{\mathbf{Q}_{i},\mathbf{Q}_{j}+\mathbf{G}_{M}}\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q}_{i},l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q}_{j},l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{Q}_{i},\mathbf{Q}_{j}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{1},l_{1},\alpha_{1}\\
s_{2},l_{2},\alpha_{2}\end{subarray}}\left[h_{\mathbf{Q}_{i},\mathbf{Q}_{j}}(\mathbf{k})\right]_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q}_{i},l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q}_{j},l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}.

The operator
\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},l,\alpha,s}
creates the continuum single-particle state associated with the refined plane-wave basis
\ket{\phi_{l\alpha s}(\vk,\vQ)}
used in the projected DFT Hamiltonian in
eq.

S2.14
. Consequently, the coefficients
t^{n_{x},n_{y}}_{l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1};\,l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2};\mathbf{G}_{M}}
in the analytical continuum Hamiltonian can be determined by matching the continuum expression for
h_{\mathbf{Q}_{i},\mathbf{Q}_{j}}(\mathbf{k})
to the numerically projected DFT Hamiltonian
h^{\mathrm{DFT}}_{\mathbf{Q}_{i},\mathbf{Q}_{j}}(\mathbf{k})
. Once these coefficients are fixed,
h_{\mathbf{Q}_{i},\mathbf{Q}_{j}}(\mathbf{k})
defines an analytic continuum Hamiltonian that can be evaluated at arbitrary
\mathbf{k}
.

We now constrain
h_{\mathbf{Q}_{i},\mathbf{Q}_{j}}(\mathbf{k})
by the symmetries of the system, such as
\{C_{3z},C_{2x},\mathcal{T}\}
in twisted AB-stacked
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
. Under a symmetry operation
g
, the continuum field operators transform as

g\,\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1}}(\mathbf{r})\,g^{-1}=\sum_{l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}\left[D(g)\right]_{l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2};\,l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1}}\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}(g\mathbf{r}),

(S2.21)

and therefore the momentum-space operators transform as

g\,\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q},l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1}}\,g^{-1}=\sum_{l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}\left[D(g)\right]_{l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2};\,l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1}}\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{g\mathbf{k},g\mathbf{Q},l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}.

(S2.22)

Here,
D(g)
is the representation matrix acting on the internal layer-orbital-spin space. Substituting
eq.

S2.22
into
eq.

S2.20
, we find

h_{g\mathbf{Q}_{i},g\mathbf{Q}_{j}}(g\mathbf{k})=D(g)\,\left[h_{\mathbf{Q}_{i},\mathbf{Q}_{j}}(\mathbf{k})\right]^{(*)}D^{\dagger}(g),

(S2.23)

where
(∗)
denotes complex conjugation only when
g
is antiunitary.

The continuum Hamiltonian
h(\mathbf{k})
in
eq.

S2.20
acts on the tensor-product space of the plane-wave sector labeled by
\mathbf{Q}
and the internal layer-orbital-spin space. For each pair
(\mathbf{Q}_{i},\mathbf{Q}_{j})
, the block
h_{\mathbf{Q}_{i},\mathbf{Q}_{j}}(\mathbf{k})
is a matrix acting on the internal space of dimension
N_{\mathrm{int}}=N_{\mathrm{layer}}N_{\mathrm{orbital}}N_{s}
. To systematically impose symmetry constraints, we construct a basis of symmetry-allowed terms for
h(\mathbf{k})
at fixed gradient order
n=n_{x}+n_{y}
and fixed moiré harmonic shell

\mathbf{G}_{M}^{(m)}\equiv\{\mathbf{G}\in\mathbf{G}_{M}\mid|\mathbf{G}|=|\mathbf{G}_{m}|\},

(S2.24)

which is closed under point-group operations, since such operations preserve both the total polynomial degree
n
and the norm
|\mathbf{G}|
.
|\mathbf{G}_{m}|
denotes the magnitude of the reciprocal vectors in the
m
-th moiré harmonic shell. This set is closed under point-group operations, since such operations preserve both the total polynomial degree
n
and the norm
|\mathbf{G}|
. At fixed
(n,m)
, the Hamiltonian is expanded in the tensor-product space

\mathcal{V}_{n,m}=\mathcal{F}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{P}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{m},

(S2.25)

where the three factors are defined as follows:

•

\mathcal{F}_{n}
is the
(n+1)
-dimensional space of homogeneous polynomials of
n
-th gradient order in a two-dimensional momentum variable
\mathbf{p}=(p_{x},p_{y})
,

\mathcal{F}_{n}=\left\{p_{x}^{n_{x}}p_{y}^{n_{y}}\,\middle|\,n_{x},n_{y}\geq 0,\;n_{x}+n_{y}=n\right\}.

(S2.26)

In the Hamiltonian,
\mathbf{p}
is evaluated as
\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{Q}_{i}
. For example, for
n=2
,

\mathcal{F}_{2}=\{p_{x}^{2},\;p_{x}p_{y},\;p_{y}^{2}\}.

(S2.27)

•

\mathcal{P}
is the space of Hermitian matrices acting on the internal layer-orbital-spin space,

\mathcal{P}=\{P_{\mu}\},

(S2.28)

whose dimension is
N_{\mathrm{int}}^{2}
. For example, if spin is the only internal degree of freedom,
\mathcal{P}
is spanned by the four Pauli matrices.

•

\mathcal{X}_{m}
is the space spanned by the
(\mathbf{Q}_{i},\mathbf{Q}_{j})
-space matrices associated with harmonics in the
m
-th shell. For each
\mathbf{G}\in\mathbf{G}_{M}^{(m)}
, we define

[X_{\mathbf{G}}]_{\mathbf{Q}_{i},\mathbf{Q}_{j}}\equiv\delta_{\mathbf{Q}_{i},\mathbf{Q}_{j}+\mathbf{G}}.

(S2.29)

The corresponding harmonic space is

\mathcal{X}_{m}=\{X_{\mathbf{G}}\mid\mathbf{G}\in\mathbf{G}_{M}^{(m)}\},

(S2.30)

whose dimension equals the number of reciprocal vectors in the
m
-th shell.

A generic term in the continuum Hamiltonian at fixed
(n,m)
is therefore a linear combination of basis elements of the form

f_{a}(\mathbf{k})\,P_{\mu}\,X_{\mathbf{G}},\qquad f_{a}(\mathbf{k})\in\mathcal{F}_{n},\;\;P_{\mu}\in\mathcal{P},\;\;X_{\mathbf{G}}\in\mathcal{X}_{m}.

(S2.31)

The symmetry operation
g
acts separately on each factor space. On
\mathcal{F}_{n}
, it acts by momentum transformation,

\hat{F}_{g}f_{a}(\mathbf{k})=f_{a}(g^{-1}\mathbf{k})=\sum_{b}f_{b}(\mathbf{k})\,\mathcal{F}_{ba}(g).

(S2.32)

where
\mathcal{F}_{ba}(g)
is the representation matrix of
g
on the space
\mathcal{F}_{n}
spanned by
\{f_{a}(\mathbf{k})\}
. On
\mathcal{P}
, it acts by conjugation with the internal representation,

\hat{P}_{g}P_{\mu}=D(g)\,P_{\mu}^{(*)}\,D^{\dagger}(g)=\sum_{\nu}P_{\nu}\,\mathcal{P}_{\nu\mu}(g),

(S2.33)

where
(∗)
again denotes complex conjugation only for antiunitary
g
, and
\mathcal{P}_{\nu\mu}(g)
is the representation matrix of
g
on the space
\mathcal{P}
spanned by
\{P_{\mu}\}
. On
\mathcal{X}_{m}
, it acts by rotating the moiré reciprocal vector,

\hat{X}_{g}X_{\mathbf{G}}=X_{g\mathbf{G}}=\sum_{\mathbf{G}^{\prime}\in\mathbf{G}_{M}^{(m)}}X_{\mathbf{G}^{\prime}}\,\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{G}^{\prime},\mathbf{G}}(g).

(S2.34)

where
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{G}^{\prime},\mathbf{G}}(g)
is the representation matrix of
g
on the space
\mathcal{X}_{m}
spanned by
\{X_{\mathbf{G}}\}
. With these definitions, the symmetry condition in
eq.

S2.23
is equivalent to requiring that the Hamiltonian matrix terms at fixed
(n,m)
be invariant under the combined action

\hat{F}_{g}\otimes\hat{P}_{g}\otimes\hat{X}_{g}.

(S2.35)

In other words, the symmetry-allowed terms are precisely those basis combinations in
\mathcal{V}_{n,m}
that transform in the trivial representation of
\hat{F}_{g}\otimes\hat{P}_{g}\otimes\hat{X}_{g}
.

The actions of point-group operations on
\mathcal{F}_{n}
and
\mathcal{X}_{m}
are fixed independently of the material. By contrast, the representation matrix
D(g)
acting on the internal layer-orbital-spin space depends on the chosen orbital basis in
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
. In the following section, we derive the corresponding representation matrices
D(g)
, taking into account both the exact symmetries of the twisted bilayer and the additional constraints inherited from the untwisted configuration
Călugăru et al. 2025
, which we refer to as zero-twist symmetries.

II.2.1
Zero-twist symmetry of
\Gamma
-valley from local stacking approximation

We consider a family of untwisted bilayer Hamiltonians
\mathcal{H}(\Delta\mathbf{R})
, where the top and bottom layers are displaced by
+\Delta\mathbf{R}/2
and
-\Delta\mathbf{R}/2
, respectively. In the AA-stacked geometry, the lattice site
(\mathbf{R},l)
is located at

\mathbf{r}^{\Delta\mathbf{R}}_{l,\mathbf{R}}=\mathbf{R}+\frac{l}{2}\Delta\mathbf{R},

(S2.36)

whereas in the AB-stacked geometry we use a convenient layer-dependent Bravais-label convention in which the lower layer is indexed by
-\mathbf{R}
, so the site
(\mathbf{R},l)
is located at

\mathbf{r}^{\Delta\mathbf{R}}_{l,\mathbf{R}}=l\,\mathbf{R}+\frac{l}{2}\Delta\mathbf{R},

(S2.37)

with
l=\pm 1
labeling the two layers. We denote by
\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},l,\alpha,s}
the corresponding lattice creation operator. The untwisted bilayer Hamiltonian can then be written as

\mathcal{H}(\Delta\mathbf{R})=\sum_{l}\mathcal{H}_{l}^{\text{sl}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{R}_{1},l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1}\\
\mathbf{R}_{2},l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}\end{subarray}}S_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}\!\left(\Delta\mathbf{R},\,\mathbf{r}^{\Delta\mathbf{R}}_{l_{2},\mathbf{R}_{2}}-\mathbf{r}^{\Delta\mathbf{R}}_{l_{1},\mathbf{R}_{1}}\right)\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}_{1},l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1}}\hat{a}_{\mathbf{R}_{2},l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}.

(S2.38)

Here

\mathcal{H}_{l}^{\text{sl}}=\sum_{\mathbf{k}}\left[h(\mathbf{k})\right]_{\alpha_{1}s_{1};\alpha_{2}s_{2}}\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},l,\alpha_{1},s_{1}}\hat{a}_{\mathbf{k},l,\alpha_{2},s_{2}}

(S2.39)

is the single-layer Hamiltonian of layer
l
. The coefficient

S_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}(\Delta\mathbf{R},\mathbf{r})

is the stacking-induced single-particle matrix element between two lattice fermions separated by
\mathbf{r}
. For
l_{1}\neq l_{2}
it describes interlayer tunneling, while for
l_{1}=l_{2}
it describes stacking-induced intralayer
corrections.

Because
\mathcal{H}(\Delta\mathbf{R})
retains the lattice translation symmetry of a monolayer,
S_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}(\Delta\mathbf{R},\mathbf{r})
depends only on the relative coordinate
\mathbf{r}
, not on the absolute positions of the two sites. Moreover, translating one layer by a primitive lattice vector leaves the local stacking unchanged, so

S_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}(\Delta\mathbf{R}+\mathbf{a}_{i},\mathbf{r})=S_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}(\Delta\mathbf{R},\mathbf{r}),\qquad i=1,2.

(S2.40)

As in Ref.
Călugăru et al. 2025
, we assume that this kernel is short-ranged in its second argument,

S_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}(\Delta\mathbf{R},\mathbf{r})\approx 0\qquad\text{for }|\mathbf{r}|\gg|\mathbf{a}_{1}|.

(S2.41)

eq.

S2.40
allows us to Fourier expand
S
in the interlayer displacement
\Delta\mathbf{R}
, while its dependence on
\mathbf{r}
can be represented in crystal momentum space. We introduce

S_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}(\Delta\mathbf{R},\mathbf{r})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\mathbf{g},\mathbf{k}}S_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}(\mathbf{g},\mathbf{k})\,e^{-i\mathbf{g}\cdot\Delta\mathbf{R}}\,e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}},

(S2.42)

where
\mathbf{g}
is a reciprocal lattice vector of the monolayer crystal,
N
is the number of monolayer unit cells and
\mathbf{k}
runs over monolayer Brillouin zone. The object that enters the
\Gamma
-valley is therefore simply

S_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}(\mathbf{g},\Gamma)\equiv S_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}(\mathbf{g},\mathbf{0}).

(S2.43)

We now turn to the twisted bilayer. As in Ref.
Călugăru et al. 2025
, the two twisted lattices are locally approximated by an untwisted bilayer with a slowly varying local displacement
\delta\mathbf{R}(\mathbf{r})
. Denoting the twisted-lattice fermion by
\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},l,\alpha,s}
, we write its physical position in the twisted lattice as
\mathbf{r}_{l,\mathbf{R}}
, which replaces the uniform-displacement position
\mathbf{r}^{\Delta\mathbf{R}}_{l,\mathbf{R}}
used in the untwisted case. The twisted Hamiltonian inherits the same two-piece structure as
eq.

S2.38
. The single-layer part carries over directly by replacing
a
with
c
in
\sum_{l}\mathcal{H}_{l}^{\text{sl}}
, while the stacking part is obtained by replacing the global displacement
\Delta\mathbf{R}
in
eq.

S2.38
with the local displacement
\delta\mathbf{R}\bigl((\mathbf{r}_{l_{1},\mathbf{R}_{1}}+\mathbf{r}_{l_{2},\mathbf{R}_{2}})/2\bigr)
evaluated at the midpoint of the two sites, a symmetric choice that treats both layers on the same footing. Because
S
is short-ranged in its second argument [
eq.

S2.41
], only site pairs with
|\mathbf{r}_{l_{2},\mathbf{R}_{2}}-\mathbf{r}_{l_{1},\mathbf{R}_{1}}|\sim|\mathbf{a}_{1}|
contribute, over which
\delta\mathbf{R}(\mathbf{r})
is effectively constant, justifying the local-untwisted approximation. The stacking part of the twisted Hamiltonian is then

\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\text{moir\'{e}}}\approx\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{R}_{1},l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1}\\
\mathbf{R}_{2},l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}\end{subarray}}S_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}\!\left(\delta\mathbf{R}\!\left(\frac{\mathbf{r}_{l_{1},\mathbf{R}_{1}}+\mathbf{r}_{l_{2},\mathbf{R}_{2}}}{2}\right),\mathbf{r}_{l_{2},\mathbf{R}_{2}}-\mathbf{r}_{l_{1},\mathbf{R}_{1}}\right)\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}_{1},l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{R}_{2},l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}.

(S2.44)

For the
\Gamma
valley, the low-energy states sit near the
\Gamma
point of the monolayer Brillouin zone. In this low-energy subspace, the lattice operator
\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},l,\alpha,s}
is represented by a slowly varying continuum field
\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l,\alpha,s}(\mathbf{r})
of
section

II.2
by the low-energy approximation

\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},l,\alpha,s}\approx\sqrt{\Omega_{0}}\,\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l,\alpha,s}(\mathbf{r}_{l,\mathbf{R}})=\sqrt{\Omega_{0}}\int\differential[2]{r}\,\delta(\mathbf{r}_{l,\mathbf{R}}-\mathbf{r})\,\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l,\alpha,s}(\mathbf{r}),

(S2.45)

where
\Omega_{0}
is the monolayer unit-cell area. We use the same symbol
c
for the lattice and continuum operators, distinguished by their arguments: the lattice operator
\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R},l,\alpha,s}
is labelled by a discrete lattice vector
\mathbf{R}
, while the continuum operator
\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l,\alpha,s}(\mathbf{r})
is a function of
the continuous position
\mathbf{r}
.

We now reduce
eq.

S2.44
to a continuum form following
Călugăru et al. 2025
. First, the short-rangedness of
S
in its second argument [
eq.

S2.41
] restricts the sum over
(\mathbf{R}_{1},\mathbf{R}_{2})
in
eq.

S2.44
to site pairs with
|\mathbf{r}_{l_{2},\mathbf{R}_{2}}-\mathbf{r}_{l_{1},\mathbf{R}_{1}}|\sim|\mathbf{a}_{1}|
, which is small compared to the scale
\Lambda^{-1}
on which the continuum field varies. Substituting
eq.

S2.45
for both lattice operators yields a factor of
\Omega_{0}
, and by the slowly-varying assumption the two continuum fields can be evaluated at a common point
\mathbf{r}
. We finally arrive at

\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\text{moir\'{e}}}\approx\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1}\\
l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}\end{subarray}}\int\differential[2]{r}\sum_{\mathbf{R}_{2}}S_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}\!\left(\delta\mathbf{R}(\mathbf{r}),\,\mathbf{r}_{l_{2},\mathbf{R}_{2}}-\mathbf{r}\right)\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1}}(\mathbf{r})\,\hat{c}_{l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}(\mathbf{r}).

(S2.46)

Second, we Fourier-expand
S
via
eq.

S2.42
. The
remaining lattice sum takes the form

\sum_{\mathbf{R}_{2}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot(\mathbf{r}_{l_{2},\mathbf{R}_{2}}-\mathbf{r})}=e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}_{2}}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}_{l_{2},\mathbf{R}_{2}}}.

(S2.47)

For fixed layer
l_{2}
, the position
\mathbf{r}_{l_{2},\mathbf{R}_{2}}
is a linear function of
\mathbf{R}_{2}
plus a constant: in the AA convention
\mathbf{r}_{l_{2},\mathbf{R}_{2}}=\mathbf{R}_{2}+\text{const.}
, while in the AB convention
\mathbf{r}_{l_{2},\mathbf{R}_{2}}=l_{2}\mathbf{R}_{2}+\text{const.}
The sum over
\mathbf{R}_{2}
therefore vanishes unless
\mathbf{k}
coincides with a reciprocal-lattice vector of the monolayer Bravais lattice. Since in
eq.

S2.42
we choose
\mathbf{k}
in the first monolayer Brillouin zone, the only such momentum is
\mathbf{k}=\Gamma=\mathbf{0}
. The
\Gamma
-valley projection thus keeps only this term, for which all layer-dependent offset phases are unity and the lattice sum reduces to
\sum_{\mathbf{R}_{2}}1=N
. This collapses the double Fourier sum in
eq.

S2.42
to a single sum over
\mathbf{g}
with the
\mathbf{k}=\Gamma
coefficient
S_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}(\mathbf{g},\Gamma)
defined in
eq.

S2.43
, and we obtain

\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\text{moir\'{e}}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{g}\\
l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1},l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}\end{subarray}}\int\differential[2]{r}\,S_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}(\mathbf{g},\Gamma)\,\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1}}(\mathbf{r})\,\hat{c}_{l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}(\mathbf{r})\,e^{-i\mathbf{g}\cdot\delta\mathbf{R}(\mathbf{r})}.

(S2.48)

Using the standard relation for the local displacement field in a twisted
bilayer,

e^{-i\mathbf{g}\cdot\delta\mathbf{R}(\mathbf{r})}=e^{-2i\sin(\theta/2)(\mathbf{g}\crossproduct\hat{\mathbf{z}})\cdot\mathbf{r}}
,
we obtain

\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\text{moir\'{e}}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{g}\\
l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1},l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}\end{subarray}}\int\differential[2]{r}\,S_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}(\mathbf{g},\Gamma)\,\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1}}(\mathbf{r})\,\hat{c}_{l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}(\mathbf{r})\,e^{-2i\sin(\theta/2)(\mathbf{g}\crossproduct\hat{\mathbf{z}})\cdot\mathbf{r}}.

(S2.49)

eq.

S2.49
defines the real-space moiré
potential

V^{\Gamma}_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}(\mathbf{r})=\sum_{\mathbf{g}}S_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}(\mathbf{g},\Gamma)\,e^{-2i\sin(\theta/2)(\mathbf{g}\crossproduct\hat{\mathbf{z}})\cdot\mathbf{r}}.

(S2.50)

The phases in
eq.

S2.50
are labelled by a monolayer reciprocal vector
\mathbf{g}
, while the natural Fourier labels in the twisted system are moiré reciprocal vectors. In the generic formulation, each layer carries its own valley-centre offset
\mathbf{q}_{l}
on top of the moir’e labelling. For the
\Gamma
-valley problem the monolayer valley sits at
\mathbf{0}
and is invariant under rotation, so
\mathbf{q}_{l}=\mathbf{0}
in both layers and these offsets drop out. The monolayer reciprocal vector
\mathbf{g}
is then mapped directly onto a moiré reciprocal vector
\mathbf{G}
by the
moiré reciprocal-lattice map

-2\sin(\theta/2)\,(\mathbf{g}\crossproduct\hat{\mathbf{z}})=\mathbf{G},

(S2.51)

which is a bijection between the monolayer and moiré reciprocal lattices.

Under this identification, the moiré potential admits the Fourier expansion

V^{\Gamma}_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}(\mathbf{r})=\sum_{\mathbf{G}}\left[T^{\Gamma}_{\mathbf{G}}\right]_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}e^{i\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{r}},

(S2.52)

where
[T^{\Gamma}_{\mathbf{G}}]_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}
is the momentum-space matrix element coupling plane-wave components that differ by the moiré reciprocal vector
\mathbf{G}
. Fourier inversion over a moiré unit cell of area
\Omega
gives

\left[T^{\Gamma}_{\mathbf{G}}\right]_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}=\frac{1}{\Omega}\int\differential[2]{r}\,V^{\Gamma}_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}(\mathbf{r})\,e^{-i\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{r}}.

(S2.53)

Substituting
eq.

S2.50
into

eq.

S2.53
and inverting

eq.

S2.51
as

\mathbf{g}=\mathbf{G}\crossproduct\hat{\mathbf{z}}/[2\sin(\theta/2)]
, we obtain

\left[T^{\Gamma}_{\mathbf{G}}\right]_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}=S_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}\!\left(\frac{\mathbf{G}\crossproduct\hat{\mathbf{z}}}{2\sin(\theta/2)},\,\Gamma\right).

(S2.54)

Thus the
\Gamma
-valley moiré matrix elements
T^{\Gamma}
are in one-to-one correspondence with the Fourier components
S(\mathbf{g},\Gamma)
of the untwisted stacking kernel.

To connect
T^{\Gamma}_{\mathbf{G}}
to the
(\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2})
labelled form used elsewhere in the paper, we project the tunneling Hamiltonian onto the plane-wave mesh of
eq.

S2.18
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\Gamma}_{\text{moir\'{e}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1}\\
l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}\end{subarray}}\int\differential[2]{r}\,V^{\Gamma}_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}(\mathbf{r})\,\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1}}(\mathbf{r})\,\hat{c}_{l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}(\mathbf{r})

(S2.55)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathrm{MBZ}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2}\\
l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1}\\
l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}\end{subarray}}\left[T^{\Gamma}_{\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2}}\right]_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q}_{1},l_{1},\alpha_{1},s_{1}}\hat{c}_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{Q}_{2},l_{2},\alpha_{2},s_{2}},

with

\left[T^{\Gamma}_{\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2}}\right]_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}\;\equiv\;\left[T^{\Gamma}_{\mathbf{Q}_{1},\,\mathbf{Q}_{2}+\mathbf{G}}\right]_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}\Big|_{\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{Q}_{1}-\mathbf{Q}_{2}}=\left[T^{\Gamma}_{\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{Q}_{1}-\mathbf{Q}_{2}}\right]_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}.

(S2.56)

This is the momentum-space tunneling block that enters the matrix-element definition
[h(\mathbf{k})_{\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2}}]_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}
used elsewhere in the paper.

We now impose the zero-twist symmetry constraints by specialising the
general continuum symmetry relation derived earlier. For any symmetry

g=\{p_{g}|\tau_{g}\}
of the zero-displacement untwisted bilayer,

eq.

S2.23
states that the continuum Hamiltonian
matrix element satisfies

h_{p_{g}\mathbf{Q}_{1},\,p_{g}\mathbf{Q}_{2}}(p_{g}\mathbf{k})=D(g)\,[h_{\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2}}(\mathbf{k})]^{(*)}\,D^{\dagger}(g),

(S2.57)

where
(*)
denotes complex conjugation for antiunitary
g
. The moiré
tunneling block is the
\mathbf{k}
-independent part of
h
and equals

T^{\Gamma}_{\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2}}
, so evaluating this constraint on the tunneling
block gives

T^{\Gamma}_{p_{g}\mathbf{Q}_{1},\,p_{g}\mathbf{Q}_{2}}=D(g)\,[T^{\Gamma}_{\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2}}]^{(*)}\,D^{\dagger}(g).

(S2.58)

By
eq.

S2.56
,
T^{\Gamma}_{\mathbf{Q}_{1},\mathbf{Q}_{2}}
depends only on
the difference
\mathbf{G}\equiv\mathbf{Q}_{1}-\mathbf{Q}_{2}
, and

p_{g}\mathbf{Q}_{1}-p_{g}\mathbf{Q}_{2}=p_{g}\mathbf{G}
, so the constraint reduces to a condition on
the
\mathbf{G}
-labelled coefficients,

T^{\Gamma}_{p_{g}\mathbf{G}}=D(g)\,[T^{\Gamma}_{\mathbf{G}}]^{(*)}\,D^{\dagger}(g).

(S2.59)

Note that the fractional translation
\tau_{g}
of a possibly nonsymmorphic

g
does not enter: at the lattice level it would appear through a Bloch
phase
e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\tau_{g}}
, which is trivial at the valley centre

\mathbf{k}=\Gamma=\mathbf{0}
on which the continuum theory is built.

We next factor
D(g)
into its action on the layer index and on the
internal single-layer degrees of freedom
(\alpha,s)
,

[D(g)]_{l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2};\,l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1}}=\delta_{l_{2},\,\epsilon_{g}l_{1}}\,[D^{\text{sl}}(g)]_{\alpha_{2}s_{2};\,\alpha_{1}s_{1}},

(S2.60)

where
\epsilon_{g}=+1
if
g
preserves the layer index and
\epsilon_{g}=-1

if it exchanges the two layers. In this notation

eq.

S2.59
reads

\left[T^{\Gamma}_{p_{g}\mathbf{G}}\right]_{\epsilon_{g}l_{1},\alpha^{\prime}_{1}s^{\prime}_{1};\,\epsilon_{g}l_{2},\alpha^{\prime}_{2}s^{\prime}_{2}}=\sum_{\alpha_{1},s_{1},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}\left[D^{\text{sl}}(g)\right]_{\alpha^{\prime}_{1}s^{\prime}_{1};\alpha_{1}s_{1}}\left[T^{\Gamma}_{\mathbf{G}}\right]^{(*)}_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};\,l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}\left[D^{\text{sl}}(g)\right]^{*}_{\alpha^{\prime}_{2}s^{\prime}_{2};\alpha_{2}s_{2}}.

(S2.61)

Finally, substituting
T^{\Gamma}_{\mathbf{G}}=S(\mathbf{g},\Gamma)
from

eq.

S2.54
, we track how
p_{g}
acts on the
microscopic reciprocal vector
\mathbf{g}
through the moiré map

eq.

S2.51
. Applying
p_{g}
to

\mathbf{G}=-2\sin(\theta/2)\,(\mathbf{g}\crossproduct\hat{\mathbf{z}})
and using

p_{g}\hat{\mathbf{z}}=\epsilon_{g}\hat{\mathbf{z}}
(the point-group element flips

\hat{\mathbf{z}}
precisely when it exchanges the two layers), we find

p_{g}\mathbf{G}=-2\sin(\theta/2)\,(\epsilon_{g}p_{g}\mathbf{g}\crossproduct\hat{\mathbf{z}}),

(S2.62)

so
p_{g}\mathbf{G}
corresponds under the moiré map to the microscopic vector

\epsilon_{g}p_{g}\mathbf{g}
, i.e.,

T^{\Gamma}_{p_{g}\mathbf{G}}=S(\epsilon_{g}p_{g}\mathbf{g},\Gamma)
. This yields the
zero-twist symmetry constraint

\sum_{\alpha_{1},s_{1},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}\left[D^{\text{sl}}(g)\right]_{\alpha^{\prime}_{1}s^{\prime}_{1};\alpha_{1}s_{1}}S^{(*)}_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}(\mathbf{g},\Gamma)\left[D^{\text{sl}}(g)\right]^{*}_{\alpha^{\prime}_{2}s^{\prime}_{2};\alpha_{2}s_{2}}=S_{\epsilon_{g}l_{1},\alpha^{\prime}_{1}s^{\prime}_{1};\,\epsilon_{g}l_{2},\alpha^{\prime}_{2}s^{\prime}_{2}}(\epsilon_{g}p_{g}\mathbf{g},\Gamma),

(S2.63)

where the superscript
(*)
means complex conjugation if
g
is
antiunitary.

In the AA-stacked structure, the untwist symmetry absent in twist configuration is inversion
\mathcal{I}
. Since
p_{\mathcal{I}}\mathbf{g}=-\mathbf{g}
and
\epsilon_{\mathcal{I}}=-1
, one has

\epsilon_{\mathcal{I}}p_{\mathcal{I}}\mathbf{g}=\mathbf{g}
, and therefore

\sum_{\alpha_{1},s_{1},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}\left[D^{\text{sl}}(\mathcal{I})\right]_{\alpha^{\prime}_{1}s^{\prime}_{1};\alpha_{1}s_{1}}S^{\text{AA}}_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}(\mathbf{g},\Gamma)\left[D^{\text{sl}}(\mathcal{I})\right]^{*}_{\alpha^{\prime}_{2}s^{\prime}_{2};\alpha_{2}s_{2}}=S^{\text{AA}}_{-l_{1},\alpha^{\prime}_{1}s^{\prime}_{1};\,-l_{2},\alpha^{\prime}_{2}s^{\prime}_{2}}(\mathbf{g},\Gamma).

(S2.64)

In the AB-stacked structure, the untwist symmetry absent in twist configuration is
M_{z}
. Since
p_{M_{z}}\mathbf{g}=\mathbf{g}
for an in-plane reciprocal vector and
\epsilon_{M_{z}}=-1
, one finds
\epsilon_{M_{z}}p_{M_{z}}\mathbf{g}=-\mathbf{g}
, so that

\sum_{\alpha_{1},s_{1},\alpha_{2},s_{2}}\left[D^{\text{sl}}(\mathcal{M}_{z})\right]_{\alpha^{\prime}_{1}s^{\prime}_{1};\alpha_{1}s_{1}}S^{\text{AB}}_{l_{1}\alpha_{1}s_{1};l_{2}\alpha_{2}s_{2}}(\mathbf{g},\Gamma)\left[D^{\text{sl}}(\mathcal{M}_{z})\right]^{*}_{\alpha^{\prime}_{2}s^{\prime}_{2};\alpha_{2}s_{2}}=S^{\text{AB}}_{-l_{1},\alpha^{\prime}_{1}s^{\prime}_{1};\,-l_{2},\alpha^{\prime}_{2}s^{\prime}_{2}}(-\mathbf{g},\Gamma).

(S2.65)

Consequently, the untwist
\mathcal{I}
acts like
\mathcal{M}_{z}
in twisted AA-stacking, while the untwist
\mathcal{M}_{z}
acts like
\mathcal{I}
in twisted AA-stacking.

II.2.2
Exact and zero-twist symmetry constraints in twisted bilayer
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}

When constructing the basis of continuum model for twisted AA/AB-stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, we consider two layers, two orbitals (
i.e.
,
\alpha\in\{P,\bar{s}\}
) and spin degree of freedom, as discussed in
section

II.2
. For twisted AA–
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, whose exact symmetries are
C_{3z}
,
C_{2x}
and
\mathcal{T}
.
The
\mathcal{I}
in untwist AA-stacked bilayer acts as
{\mathcal{M}}_{z}
as derived in
section

II.2.1
.
Considering the spinful molecular orbital
P
with
J_{z}=\pm\frac{3}{2}
, the representation matrices for both exact and zero-twist symmetries can be obtained by element-wise products (“
\circ
”) of the orbital and spin parts,

\displaystyle D^{P}(C_{3z})=e^{-i\frac{2\pi}{3}\sigma_{z}}\circ e^{-i\frac{\pi}{3}s_{z}}=-s_{0},\quad D^{P}(C_{2x})=\sigma_{x}\circ-is_{x}=-is_{x},

(S2.66)

\displaystyle D^{P}(\mathcal{T})=\sigma_{x}\circ-is_{y}\kappa=-is_{y}\kappa,\quad D^{P}(\mathcal{M}_{z})=\sigma_{0}\circ-is_{z}=-is_{z}.

where
\sigma_{i}
and
s_{i}
act in orbital and spin spaces, respectively, and
\kappa
denotes complex conjugation. Similarly, the representation matrices for the spinful molecular orbital
\bar{s}
with
J_{z}=\pm\frac{1}{2}
are

\displaystyle D^{\bar{s}}(C_{3z})=e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}\sigma_{z}}\circ e^{-i\frac{\pi}{3}s_{z}}=e^{i\frac{\pi}{3}s_{z}},\quad D^{\bar{s}}(C_{2x})=\sigma_{x}\circ-is_{x}=-is_{x},

(S2.67)

\displaystyle D^{\bar{s}}(\mathcal{T})=\sigma_{x}\circ-is_{y}\kappa=-is_{y}\kappa,\quad D^{\bar{s}}(\mathcal{M}_{z})=\sigma_{0}\circ-is_{z}=-is_{z}.

We order the basis in the order of layer, orbital, and spin,

\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l,\alpha,s}=\{\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{t,P,\uparrow},\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{t,P,\downarrow},\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{t,\bar{s},\uparrow},\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{t,\bar{s},\downarrow},\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{b,P,\uparrow},\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{b,P,\downarrow},\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{b,\bar{s},\uparrow},\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{b,\bar{s},\downarrow}\},

(S2.68)

Therefore, the representation matrices for
\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l,\alpha,s}
are

\displaystyle D^{\text{AA-{}$\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}$}}(C_{3z})=\tau_{0}\otimes\left(-s_{0}\oplus e^{i\frac{\pi}{3}s_{z}}\right),\quad D^{\text{AA-{}$\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}$}}(C_{2x})=\tau_{x}\otimes\left(-is_{x}\oplus-is_{x}\right)

(S2.69)

\displaystyle D^{\text{AA-{}$\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}$}}(\mathcal{T})=\tau_{0}\otimes\left(-is_{y}\kappa\oplus-is_{y}\kappa\right),\quad D^{\text{AA-{}$\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}$}}(\mathcal{M}_{z})=\tau_{x}\otimes\left(-is_{z}\oplus-is_{z}\right).

Here,
\tau_{i}
(for
i=0,x,y,z
) are identity/Pauli matrices acting in the layer subspace.

For AB-stacking case, the exact symmetries are
C_{3z}
,
C_{2y}
and
\mathcal{T}
.
\mathcal{M}_{z}
in untwist AB bilayer becomes zero-twist symmetry
\mathcal{I}
in twisted configuration. Listing only what differs from the AA case, the representation matrices for the continuum basis
\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l,\alpha,s}
are

\displaystyle D^{\text{AB-{}$\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}$}}(C_{2y})=\tau_{x}\otimes\left(-is_{y}\oplus-is_{y}\right),\quad D^{\text{AB-{}$\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}$}}(\mathcal{I})=\tau_{x}\otimes\left(s_{0}\oplus s_{0}\right).

(S2.70)

II.2.3
Exact and zero-twist symmetry constraints in twisted bilayer
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}

The exact and zero-twist symmetries in twisted
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
are identical to those in
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
. However, in
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, we keep only the
\bar{P}
orbital in our continuum model basis since the
s
molecular orbital basis only contributes to deeper moiré bands as discussed in
section

II.1
. So we drop the orbital index
\alpha
in
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
. Besides, unlike
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
where
P
and
\bar{s}
are inversion-odd,molecular orbitals
R
in
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
have even parity under inversion. Consequently, although the symmetry groups for the AA/AB-stacked
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
are the same, their corresponding representation matrices slightly differ. For twisted AA-stacked
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, the continuum basis
\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l,s}
is

\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l,s}=\{\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{t,\uparrow},\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{t,\downarrow},\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{b,\uparrow},\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{b,\downarrow}\},

(S2.71)

the representation matrices reads,

\displaystyle D^{\text{AB-{}$\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}$}}(C_{3z})=\tau_{0}\otimes s_{0},\quad D^{\text{AB-{}$\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}$}}(C_{2x})=\tau_{x}\otimes-is_{x},

(S2.72)

\displaystyle D^{\text{AB-{}$\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}$}}(\mathcal{T})=\tau_{0}\otimes-is_{y}\kappa,\quad D^{\text{AB-{}$\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}$}}(\mathcal{M}_{z})=\tau_{x}\otimes-is_{z}.

In AB-stacked case, the representation matrices for
C_{2y}
and
\mathcal{I}
are,

\displaystyle D^{\text{AB-{}$\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}$}}(C_{2y})=\tau_{x}\otimes is_{y},\quad D^{\text{AB-{}$\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}$}}(\mathcal{I})=\tau_{x}\otimes-is_{0}.

(S2.73)

II.3
Continuum model extracted/fitted from
ab initio
Hamiltonian

In the above section, we outlined the symmetry constraints and representation matrices for the continuum basis
\hat{c}^{\dagger}_{l,\alpha,s}
in twisted AA/AB-stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
. By applying constraints of exact and/or zero-twist symmetries, we obtain the independent symmetry-allowed terms of
h_{\mathbf{Q}_{i},\mathbf{Q}_{j}}(\mathbf{k})
. Since the projected DFT Hamiltonian (
h^{\mathrm{DFT}}(\mathbf{k})
in
eq.

S2.14
) is equivalent to the symmetry-constrained continuum model in the continuum limit, we can parametrize the continuum Hamiltonian in terms of the independent symmetry-allowed terms and extract the numerical values of their coefficients from the projected DFT Hamiltonian
h^{\mathrm{DFT}}(\mathbf{k})

Călugăru et al. 2025
;
Zhang et al. 2024
.
We built two models of different complexity. First, we construct a continuum model with dozens to hundreds of parameters that reproduces both band dispersions and wavefunctions of the top six/two spinful bands in
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
/
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
. To assess the wavefunction agreement, we define the overlap between the DFT wavefunction
|\psi_{\text{dft}}\rangle
and the continuum model wavefunction
|\psi_{\text{model}}\rangle
. Note that because the isolated low-energy bands are almost doubly degenerate, each pair of nearly-degenerate bands is grouped into one set.
The overlap for
m
-set of bands at
\mathbf{k}
is defined as follows,

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{m}(\mathbf{k})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{\operatorname{Tr}\left[P_{m,\text{DFT}}P_{m,\text{model}}\right]}{2}},

(S2.74)

\displaystyle P_{m,\text{DFT/model}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in m}|\psi_{i,\text{DFT/model}}\rangle\langle\psi_{i,\text{DFT/model}}|.

Here,
P_{m,\text{dft/model}}
is the projector defined using the two degenerated bands in set
m
. The full models achieve overlaps above 99% and 95% for
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, respectively.
Second, to gain analytical insight, we apply the step-wise regression procedure of Ref.
Călugăru et al. 2025
to reduce parameters of the full model and obtain a simplified model with less than 10 parameters (including effective mass).
We start from the continuum model with dozens to hundreds of parameters, and test the effect of removing each parameter individually, while keeping the others fixed. The parameter whose removal increases the loss the most is discarded, and the reduced model is then refit. Repeating this procedure produces a nested set of models with progressively fewer parameters. We then choose the simplest model that still achieves the desired accuracy.
The simplified model still matches the dispersions and wavefunctions of isolated bands, with overlaps exceeding 93% and 75% for
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
and
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, respectively. In what follows, we detail both continuum models for each material.

II.3.1
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}

Figure S12:
The comparison of band structure between the first-harmonic continuum model and DFT for twisted
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
. (a-c) Band structure for twisted AA-stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
from

to

. (d-f) Band structure for twisted AB-stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
from
5.09\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}
to
3.89\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}
. The black line is the result of the first-harmonic continuum model, and the red dots are DFT results with color intensity reflecting the overlap between the DFT and continuum-model wavefunctions.

For
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
system, we consider up to first harmonic (
\left|\mathbf{Q}_{i}-\mathbf{Q}_{j}\right|=\left|b_{M1}\right|
) for
\mathbf{k}
-independent terms (
n_{x}+n_{y}=0
), and
\mathbf{Q}_{i}=\mathbf{Q}_{j}
for the
\mathbf{k}
-dependent terms (
i.e.
,
1\leq n_{x}+n_{y}\leq 2
). Because the model contains two layers, two orbitals (
P
and
\bar{s}
molecular orbitals) and two spin flavors, the first-harmonic continuum Hamiltonians constrained by the exact symmetries involve 65 and 61 independent terms for twisted AA-stacked and AB-stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, respectively, as listed in
tables

S9
and
S10
. We extract the parameters by linear least-squares fitting and compare the full continuum model with the projected DFT Hamiltonian in
fig.

S12
from twist angles
5.09\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}
to
3.89\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}
(AA on the top row, AB on the bottom row). The dispersions match closely and the wavefunction overlap exceeds 99% for the top three sets of bands. Besides, the
C_{3}
eigenvalues of the continuum model at
C_{3}
invariant high symmetry points agree with the DFT results shown in
table

S2
and
table

S3
.

For the first-harmonic model with 65 and 61 parameters for twisted AA- and AB-stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, we calculate the Wilson loops for the top three sets of bands at
3.89\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}
. As shown in
figs.

S13(a)
and
S13(g)
, the first set of bands has a nontrivial winding number and exhibits a quantum spin Hall (QSH) state for both stackings. On the contrary, both the second and third sets of band have trivial
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
indices as shown in
figs.

S13(b)
,
S13(c)
,
S13(h)
and
S13(i)
.
We quantify the breaking of spin-
\mathrm{U}(1)
symmetry in the continuum Hamiltonian
h(\mathbf{k})
by

\Delta_{H}(\mathbf{k})=\frac{\|[h(\mathbf{k}),s_{z}]\|_{F}}{2\left\|h(\mathbf{k})-\dfrac{\Tr[h(\mathbf{k})]}{N}\mathbb{I}\right\|_{F}},

(S2.75)

where
N
is the dimension of
h(\mathbf{k})
. For both twisted AA- and AB-stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, the maximum value of
\Delta_{H}(\mathbf{k})
is below
2\%
. This indicates that spin-
\mathrm{U}(1)
symmetry is only weakly broken, so that spin-projected geometric quantities are still well defined to a good approximation.
For the
n
-th isolated two-band set, we define the projector

P_{n}(\mathbf{k})=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{2}|u_{n\alpha\mathbf{k}}\rangle\langle u_{n\alpha\mathbf{k}}|,

(S2.76)

where
|u_{n\alpha\mathbf{k}}\rangle
are the Bloch eigenstates spanning that isolated set. We then diagonalize the projected spin operator

P_{n}(\mathbf{k})s_{z}P_{n}(\mathbf{k})\,|u^{s}_{n\mathbf{k}}\rangle=\lambda_{n}^{s}(\mathbf{k})\,|u^{s}_{n\mathbf{k}}\rangle,\qquad s=\uparrow,\downarrow,

(S2.77)

and use the resulting spin-adapted states
|u^{s}_{n\mathbf{k}}\rangle
to define the spin-projected Berry connection

\mathcal{A}^{s}_{n,i}(\mathbf{k})=i\left\langle u^{s}_{n\mathbf{k}}\middle|\partial_{k_{i}}u^{s}_{n\mathbf{k}}\right\rangle.

(S2.78)

The corresponding spin-projected Wilson-loop phase is

\theta_{n}^{s}(k_{a})=\mathrm{Im}\,\log\left[\exp\!\left(i\oint dk_{b}\,\mathcal{A}^{s}_{n,b}(k_{a},k_{b})\right)\right].

(S2.79)

The spin-projected Wilson loops for the topmost band set, shown in
figs.

S13(d)
and
S13(j)
, yield a spin Chern number
|C_{s}|=1
for both AA and AB stackings. For the second and third band sets, shown in
figs.

S13(e)
,
S13(f)
,
S13(k)
and
S13(l)
, the spin-projected Wilson loops yield
|C_{s}|=2
, even though the corresponding
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
indices are trivial.

Similarly, the spin-projected Berry curvature is defined as

\Omega_{n}^{s}(\mathbf{k})=\partial_{k_{x}}\mathcal{A}^{s}_{n,y}(\mathbf{k})-\partial_{k_{y}}\mathcal{A}^{s}_{n,x}(\mathbf{k}).

(S2.80)

The Berry-curvature distributions
\Omega_{n}(\mathbf{k})
of the top three isolated band sets in the first-harmonic continuum model for both stackings are shown in
fig.

S15
. For easier comparison across different twist angles, we plot the dimensionless quantity
\Omega_{n}\mathcal{A}_{\rm MBZ}
, where
\mathcal{A}_{\rm MBZ}
is the area of the first moiré Brillouin zone. Because
{C_{2z}\mathcal{T}}
/
C_{2z}\mathcal{T}
is only an approximate symmetry in first-harmonic model of AA/AB-stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, the ordinary Berry curvature is nonzero but suppressed when compared with the spin-projected Berry curvature
\Omega_{n}^{s}(\mathbf{k})
.

We next examine the real-space charge distribution of the isolated bands. The local density of states (LDOS) for the top three sets in both AA- and AB-stacked twisted bilayer
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
are shown in

. The hexagon marks the moiré Wigner–Seitz cell, the two arrows indicate the moiré lattice vectors, and color encodes intensity from blue (low) to red (high). The LDOS distribution exhibit the crystalline symmetry for both stackings: (a) within each layer, the LDOS in both AA-stacked and AB-stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
show the
C_{3z}
symmetry, (b) the top layer (first row) is related to the bottom layer (second row) by the exact symmetry
C_{2x}
and
C_{2y}
in AA-stacked and AB-stacked configuration, respectively. In
fig.

, the LDOS of the topmost set of band is relatively extended with minimum at the
1b
Wyckoff position. The second set of band is maximized at
1b
Wyckoff position and forms a triangular lattice as illustrated in
fig.

. The third set exhibits a kagome pattern with sites in the middle between the
1a
and
1c
Wyckoff positions, as depicted in
fig.

. Furthermore, the AA and AB LDOS are related by a rigid fractional moiré translation: shifting the AB LDOS by
\tfrac{2}{3}\mathbf{a}_{M,1}+\tfrac{1}{3}\mathbf{a}_{M,2}
maps the patterns of the top three sets in AB onto those in AA as shown by
figs.

S14(d)
,
S14(e)
and
S14(f)
. Finally, the LDOS patterns display approximate twofold symmetry about
z
. More specifically, LDOS in AA are nearly invariant under
C_{2z}
operation with in-plane fractional translation,
i.e.
,
\tilde{C}_{2z}=\{C_{2z}\,|\,(\frac{2}{3},\,\frac{1}{3})\}
, whereas in AB they are nearly invariant under
C_{2z}
. Neither of them is an exact symmetry of the system.

To clarify the relation between AA and AB stackings and the approximate symmetries suggested by the LDOS, we reduce the number of parameters by applying the step-wise regression to the first harmonic model at
3.89\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}

Călugăru et al. 2025
. The simplified models of AA-stacked and AB-stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
at
3.89\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}
have the same analytical expression as follows but different numerical values for parameters,

\displaystyle h^{\text{AA/AB}}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\hbar^{2}\bm{\nabla}^{2}}{2m_{1}^{*}}\tau_{0}\otimes\Gamma_{00}-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m^{*}_{2}}\tau_{0}\otimes\left[(\nabla_{x}^{2}-\nabla_{y}^{2})\Gamma_{x0}+2\nabla_{x}\nabla_{y}\Gamma_{yz}\right]

(S2.81)

\displaystyle+V_{1}\tau_{0}\otimes\Gamma_{z0}+V_{2}\tau_{z}\otimes\sum_{\alpha=\pm 1}e^{i\alpha\phi_{1}}\left[\Gamma_{yz}e^{i\alpha\bm{g}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{r}}+\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Gamma_{x0}-\frac{1}{2}\Gamma_{yz}\right)e^{i\alpha\bm{g}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{r}}-\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\Gamma_{x0}+\frac{1}{2}\Gamma_{yz}\right)e^{i\alpha\bm{g}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{r}}\right]

\displaystyle+w_{1}\tau_{x}\otimes\Gamma_{00}+w_{2}\tau_{x}\otimes\Gamma_{00}\sum_{i=j}^{3}\sum_{\alpha=\pm 1}e^{i\alpha\left(\phi_{2}+\bm{g}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{r}\right)},

Here, we use
V_{i}
and
w_{i}
to denote the interlayer and intralayer coupling parameters, respectively. We define
\Gamma_{ij}=\sigma_{i}\otimes s_{j}
, where
\sigma_{i}
and
s_{i}
(
i=0,x,y,z
) are the identity/Pauli matrices acting in the molecular-orbital and spin spaces. The first-harmonic moiré reciprocal vectors are
\bm{g}_{i}=C_{3z}^{i-1}\mathbf{b}_{M,1}
with
i=1,2,3
. From the simplified model, we can notice there’s an emergent symmetry
\tilde{C}_{2z}/C_{2z}

The numerical values of all parameters are listed in
table

S4
. Moreover,
\frac{1}{m_{\pm}}=\frac{1}{m_{1}^{*}}\pm\frac{1}{m_{2}^{*}}
describes the effective masses of the two molecular-orbital branches: the
J_{z}=\pm\frac{3}{2}
branch, corresponding to
P
molecular orbital with
\bar{\Gamma}_{6}\bar{\Gamma}_{7}
, and the
J_{z}=\pm\frac{1}{2}
branch, corresponding to
\bar{s}
molecular orbital with
\bar{\Gamma}_{9}
. As shown in
fig.

S5(f)
, these two branches have different effective masses. To assess the fidelity of the simplified model, we compare its dispersion with DFT and compute the overlap with DFT wavefunctions in
figs.

S17(a)
and
S17(e)
. For the top three band sets, the overlap exceeds 93% along the high symmetry line. We also plot the regular and spin-projected Wilson loop for the simplified models in
figs.

S17(b)
and
S17(f)
, where the winding numbers match those of the full model, with slight spectral differences arising from the small gap between band sets around
K_{M}
. The LDOS of the simplified model in
figs.

S17(c)
and
S17(g)
exhibits the
\tilde{C}_{2z}/C_{2z}
symmetry more prominently. The spin-projected Berry curvature for the top three sets (
figs.

S17(d)
and
S17(h)
) is concentrated near
K_{M}
, and the two spin channels have equal magnitude and opposite sign.

\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}

\tilde{C}_{2z}/{C_{2z}}
is only an approximate symmetry in twisted AA/AB-stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
, the Ordinary Berry curvature is not zero but much smaller compared to the spin-projected Berry curvature.

at

. Note that
\phi_{i}=0
in AB-stacking is required by
C_{2y}
.

((a))

((b))

((c))

((d))

Figure S16:
Interlayer-distance fields for twisted AA- and AB-stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
. (a)
d_{\mathrm{AA}}(\mathbf{r})
(b)
d_{\mathrm{AB}}(\mathbf{r})
(c) the fitted translated field
\alpha+\beta\,d_{\mathrm{AA}}(\mathbf{r}+\bm{\tau}_{0})
and (d) the residual
\varepsilon(\mathbf{r})
. Here
\bm{\tau}_{0}=\tfrac{2}{3}\mathbf{a}_{M,1}+\tfrac{1}{3}\mathbf{a}_{M,2}
, and the black hexagon denotes the moiré Wigner-Seitz cell. The agreement between the AB field and the translated AA field shows that the two corrugation patterns are approximately related by an emergent fractional translation.

The analytical form of the simplified continuum model clarifies these observations. From
eq.

S2.81
, the AA and AB models share the same functional structure. If we round the parameters in
table

S4
so that
\phi^{AA}_{i}=4\pi/3
,
V^{AA}_{i}=V^{AB}_{i}
, and
w^{AA}_{i}=w^{AB}_{i}
, the moiré potential of twisted AA and AB-stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
is related by fractional moiré lattice translation
T_{\tau_{0}}
with
\tau_{0}=\tfrac{2}{3}\mathbf{a}_{M,1}+\tfrac{1}{3}\mathbf{a}_{M,2}
. This is because under the fractional translation
\mathbf{r}\to\mathbf{r}+\frac{2}{3}\mathbf{a}_{M1}+\frac{1}{3}\mathbf{a}_{M2}
, the three first-harmonic factors acquire the same phase
e^{i\alpha 4\pi/3}
, due to
\mathbf{g}_{1}\cdot\mathbf{r}_{0}=\mathbf{g}_{2}\cdot\mathbf{r}_{0}=\mathbf{g}_{3}\cdot\mathbf{r}_{0}=4\pi/3\mod 2\pi
. This explains the similarity of LDOS of the top three band sets in AA and AB that only differ by
\tau_{0}
. Moreover, the combined symmetry
\tilde{C}_{2z}\mathcal{T}
and
C_{2z}\mathcal{T}
in twisted AA and AB stacked
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
together with the spin-
\mathrm{U}(1)
symmetry enforce the doubly degenerate bands as shown in
fig.

S17
), and the equal-magnitude but opposite-sign spin-projected Berry curvatures as shown in
fig.

S17
).

The emergent relation between the simplified AA and AB models can be traced to the local-stacking dependence of the interlayer corrugation. In the convention where the AB structure is compared to a translated AA pattern, we fit the interlayer-distance field as

d_{\mathrm{AB}}(\mathbf{r})\approx\alpha+\beta\,d_{\mathrm{AA}}(\mathbf{r}+\bm{\tau}_{0}),\qquad\bm{\tau}_{0}=\frac{2}{3}\mathbf{a}_{M,1}+\frac{1}{3}\mathbf{a}_{M,2}.

(S2.82)

Here
\alpha
accounts for the difference in average interlayer spacing, while
\beta
captures a small renormalization of the corrugation amplitude. To quantify the remaining mismatch after removing the optimal translation, offset, and amplitude rescaling, we define the residual field

\varepsilon(\mathbf{r})=d_{\mathrm{AB}}(\mathbf{r})-\bigl[\alpha+\beta\,d_{\mathrm{AA}}(\mathbf{r}+\bm{\tau}_{0})\bigr],

(S2.83)

and evaluate its root-mean-square value on the moiré sampling grid,

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rms}}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\varepsilon(\mathbf{r}_{i})^{2}}.

(S2.84)

We also report the Pearson correlation coefficient between the target field
d_{\mathrm{AB}}(\mathbf{r})
and the fitted field

d_{\mathrm{fit}}(\mathbf{r})=\alpha+\beta\,d_{\mathrm{AA}}(\mathbf{r}+\bm{\tau}_{0})
,
defined as

\rho=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[d_{\mathrm{AB}}(\mathbf{r}_{i})-\overline{d}_{\mathrm{AB}}\right]\left[d_{\mathrm{fit}}(\mathbf{r}_{i})-\overline{d}_{\mathrm{fit}}\right]}{\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[d_{\mathrm{AB}}(\mathbf{r}_{i})-\overline{d}_{\mathrm{AB}}\right]^{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[d_{\mathrm{fit}}(\mathbf{r}_{i})-\overline{d}_{\mathrm{fit}}\right]^{2}}},

(S2.85)

where the overline denotes the average over the moiré sampling grid. A value of
\rho
close to unity indicates that the translated and affinely rescaled AA field reproduces the spatial pattern of the AB field very well, even if a small residual mismatch remains.
For the relaxed
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
structures studied here, we obtain
\bm{\tau}_{0}\approx(2/3,\,1/3)
in fractional moiré coordinates,
\alpha\approx 0.851~\text{\AA}
,
\beta\approx 0.861
, and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rms}}\approx 0.040~\text{\AA}
, with correlation
\approx 0.937
. This shows that the AA and AB structures share essentially the same leading moiré corrugation texture up to a shifted origin, while the residual field measures the remaining higher-harmonic and relaxation-induced corrections, so the
\bm{\tau}_{0}
relation is emergent rather than exact.

Figure S17:
Electronic structure and topology from the simplified model for twisted bilayer
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
at
3.89\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}
. Panels (a–d) show AA-stacked and panels (e–h) show AB-stacked. (a,e) Band structures with black lines for the simplified continuum model, colored dots for DFT, and dot intensity indicating the wavefunction overlap between DFT and simplified model. (b,f) Wilson loops for the top three isolated band sets, Regular on the upper panels and spin-projected on the lower panels. (c,g) Layer-resolved LDOS for the top three band sets. II.3.2
AB-stacked
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}

For the numerically exact model of twisted AB-stacked
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
at
4.41\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}
, we include up to the second harmonic for the
\mathbf{k}
-independent and linear terms (
0\leq n_{x}+n_{y}\leq 1,\ \lvert\mathbf{Q}_{i}-\mathbf{Q}_{j}\rvert\leq\lvert\mathbf{b}_{M1}+\mathbf{b}_{M2}\rvert
) and restrict to
\mathbf{Q}_{i}=\mathbf{Q}_{j}
for the quadratic terms (
n_{x}+n_{y}=2
). Imposing the exact symmetries yields 61 independent terms as listed in
table

S11
. We fit their coefficients to the reduced DFT Hamiltonian and compare the resulting continuum model with DFT in
fig.

S18(a)
. The top two spinful bands of the model track the DFT dispersions, and the minimum wavefunction overlap exceeds 93%. The
C_{3}
eigenvalues at
C_{3}
-invariant momenta also match the DFT results in
table

S1
. Note that we restrict to the top two spinful bands when extracting parameters from DFT reduced Hamiltonian. Higher-energy moiré bands receive sizable weight from a monolayer subvalley near
\Gamma
along the
\Gamma\!-\!M
line,
i.e.
, a non–high-symmetry-point (NHSP)s valley (See
figs.

S6(b)
and
S6(e)
). Because this NHSP valley lies close in energy to
\Gamma
-valley, folding the monolayer bands into the moiré Brillouin zone at
4.41\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}
produces extra low-energy bands in
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
that are absent in folded
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
at
3.89\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolDegree}
(
figs.

S18(b)
and
S18(c)
). These additional bands originate from the NHSP subvalley, drive strong deviations from a simple parabolic kinetic approximation, and make stable parameter extraction difficult if more bands are included.

We evaluate the LDOS of the topmost band set for the full model (
fig.

S18(d)
). The top- and bottom-layer LDOS form rings around the
1b
and
1c
positions, respectively. They are related by
C_{2y}
and also exhibit an approximate inversion (
\mathcal{I}
) symmetry in addition to the exact generators. To determine the topology, we compute the Wilson loop (the left of
fig.

S18(e)
), which shows a nontrivial winding as in AB–
\text{ZrS}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
. Given the approximate spin-
\mathrm{U}(1)
symmetry present both in the projected DFT Hamiltonian and full continuum model, we also compute spin-projected Wilson loops (the right of
fig.

S18(e)
). The spin-up and spin-down sectors have winding numbers
+1
and
-1
, consistent with the
C_{3}
eigenvalues in
table

S1
. The spin-projected Berry curvature for the top band set (middle and right of
fig.

S18(f)
) is concentrated near
K_{M}
line and nearly cancels between spins, as seen in the total Berry curvature (left of
fig.

S18(f)
).

To gain a deep understanding of the system, we reduce the number of parameters and obtain a simplified analytic model. Inspecting the projected DFT Hamiltonian, we find the zero-twist inversion symmetry holds to within about 1% for the top band set, which explains the additional
\mathcal{I}
observed in the LDOS of the full model. We therefore impose
\mathcal{I}
together with the approximate spin-
\mathrm{U}(1)
symmetry, and drop the
\mathbf{k}
-dependent moiré potential terms. With these simplifications, the continuum model for
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
takes the form

\displaystyle h^{\text{AB}}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\hbar^{2}\bm{\nabla}^{2}}{2m_{1}^{*}}\tau_{0}\otimes s_{0}+V_{1}\tau_{0}\otimes s_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\sum_{\alpha=\pm 1}e^{i\alpha\bm{g}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{r}}

(S2.86)

\displaystyle+i\alpha V_{2}\tau_{z}\otimes s_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\sum_{\alpha=\pm 1}e^{i\alpha\bm{g}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{r}}+V_{3}\tau_{0}\otimes s_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\sum_{\alpha=\pm 1}e^{i\alpha\bm{g}_{2j}\cdot\mathbf{r}}

\displaystyle-\frac{\hbar^{2}\bm{\nabla}^{2}}{2m^{*}_{2}}\tau_{x}\otimes s_{0}+w_{1}\tau_{x}\otimes s_{0}+w_{2}\tau_{x}\otimes s_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\sum_{\alpha=\pm 1}e^{i\alpha\bm{g}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{r}}

\displaystyle+w_{3}\tau_{x}\otimes s_{0}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\sum_{\alpha=\pm 1}e^{i\alpha\bm{g}_{2j}\cdot\mathbf{r}}+i\alpha w_{4}\tau_{y}\otimes s_{z}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\sum_{\alpha=\pm 1}e^{i\alpha\bm{g}_{2j}\cdot\mathbf{r}}.

Here, we use
V_{i}
and
w_{i}
to denote the interlayer and intralayer coupling parameters, respectively.
\tau_{i}
and
s_{i}
(
i=0,x,y,z
) are identity/Pauli matrices acting in layer and spin subspaces, respectively. The first- and second-harmonic moiré reciprocal vectors are
\bm{g}_{i}=C_{3z}^{i-1}\mathbf{b}_{M,1}
and
\bm{g}_{2i}=C_{3z}^{i-1}(\mathbf{b}_{M,1}+\mathbf{b}_{M,2})
. The numerical values of the parameters are listed in
table

S5
. Because of the nearby monolayer NHSP valley, the
\mathbf{k}
-dependent moiré terms are essential in
\text{SnSe}{\vphantom{\text{X}}}_{\smash[t]{\text{2}}}
. Omitting them causes substantial renormalization of the fitted parameters in the simplified model compared to the full continuum model. To assess the fidelity of the simplified model, we compare the dispersion with DFT and compute wavefunction overlap. As shown in
fig.

S19(a)
, the top band set reproduces the overall dispersion, and the overlaps exceed 75% along high-symmetry lines. The regular and spin-projected Wilson loops are shown in
fig.

S19(b)
, which match those of the full model.
fig.

S19(c)
show the LDOS of the simplified model, which differs slightly in detail but retains the ring-like weight around the
1b
and
1c
positions. Besides, LDOS of top and bottom layers are related by both the exact symmetry
C_{2y}
and zero-twist symmetry
\mathcal{I}
. The spin-projected Berry curvature for the topmost band set (
fig.

S19(d)
) has equal magnitude and opposite sign in the two spin channels. The approximate combined
\mathcal{I}\mathcal{T}
symmetry accounts for both the near double degeneracy of the DFT isolated band set and the nearly vanishing total Berry curvature.
